But the man who knows that he holds the world's good opinion is always forgiving, and the next second it had passed from his mind. ' A few minutes later, and he was walking briskly away. Soon the rectory lights stood out with welcoming brightness against the darkness of a stormy night. Now he was entering a narrow road, 'between high banks, crowned with trees. It was the last part of his walk home. Every 'yard of the distance was known ' to him; therefore there was no need to slacken his - speed, even on this dark night. " Listen: he A Breakfast Cup for a id 4 gasped, don't, don't for heaven's sake go into the village! Keep out of it, in mercy to me— or—or—l'm hanged if you'll have that thousand."-

asked Alec St.

"He's ead, murdered', killed! And don't look M me like that, just as if you thought I had gone clean out of my senses ! " The last words were said in a voice of indignant ex- postulation by John Brewer, as he turned to- wards the new arrival on the scene.

"it's an awful thing. if it's true. Why, there's never been hoard of before such a thing as n murder in these parts. Don't seem a respectable customer, either. Guess the crime is the work of one of his set," replied Charles Smith, as he stood suddenly still in front of the corpse. "Anyhow, we must hurry off to tell the police. There's no time to be lost." "True enough ! " And on their way to the police-station the men told all whom they met of the ghastly sight to be seen in the road near the rectory gates. And amongst those that they met was old Frankland. He listened to the news with a troubled expression, and walked on quietly for a short distance; then he stood still, thought for' a moment, and then turned hi the opposite direction to his work, and that oppo- site direction was straight to where the murdered man lay. . . . . . .

"It's him—sure enough it's him," he said, looking down at the still form. . "What's that you said ?" asked the con- stable in charge. "That it's the man who I saw having a bit of a row with the parson last night. I came upon them ' just below, where it's darkest, and they passed me and came along up here." .

"I suppose the deceased was begging." "Well, I shouldn't say that quite. He seemed to me to be A bit threatening, and the parson he didn't speak as if he were quite on the clear side of the hedge. I heard him say something about blackmailing." "You did?" "!es, I will swear to it." "You'll have to give evidence at the inquest this afternoon."

"I shouldn't like to say half a word that might get our young parson into a row; him as is going to be married so soon." " You must be a witness," was the curt reply. Then others hurried up, and excitement grew. But Franklancl ceased for once to be a loquacious man; reviling himself for hav- ing said more than was wise , already. Be- tween now and the afternoon most busy were the police gleaning evidence. It came to hand faster than they dared to hope. A five-pound banknote, found on the murdered man was traced to the rector, and lying on the ground, close by, was a handkerchief bearing the initials " A:M.," recognised as his. Then quickly from London flashed the news, by telegram, that Robert Musgrove was the rector's brother. Why had not the fact been already told by the rector himself? The police asked the question with indignation and suspicion; and proceeded at once to sub- pcena him to attend the inquest. His confession of relationship was to all a startling and unpleasant blow. Scarcely credible was it to the majority •of his parishioners. Yet more startling, more incredible, was his arrest at the inquest on the charge of murder. Before night it was the sole topic of local conversation. Not a person believed that it was otherwise than an easily cleared away accusation; one that would leave their dearly loved rector without a stain on his reputation. The first hearing before the magistrates was devoid of any stirring disclosures; the second tightened the cordon of guilt; at the third. the prisoner was sent for trial. .

A fortnight only to wait in prison; and then he stood to be tried for the murder of his brother. It was a hard-fought case. The most able counsel of the day were briefed by his solicitors; but evidence was strong against him, and it grew in strength' as the days passed, and the unhappy maw stood in the dock, listening, praying, hoping. The verdict eame as esurprise to none. It was "Guilty," with a strong recommtnda- tion to mercy. But condemned to death was Anthony Musgrove. A week later he was reprieved. That re- reprieve meant nothing to him—he had grown to long for death during the sad days of imprisonment. It was all to the girl who loved him! Did not the fact of his existence mean that on some blessed day his innocence would come to light? Guilty he was not. She knew that. God- in His mercy would cause that innocence to be proved in the near future, she told herself, a hundred limes a day.

But weeks grew into months, months into years, and still under a load of disgrace and shame, the unhappy man lingered en in penal srvittule. Meanwhile Alec St. John grew wealthy. Fifteen thousand a year was the income all stated he now had; and approximately accu- rate was this statement. Mr. and Mrs. Thorpe heard it, and believed it, and invited him more frequently to their house, and placed within his power greater facilities for seeing. _lrene:

Of these facilities he never failed to avail himself.

" If the dear girl were once married to him she would entirely cease to think of that wicked murderer," soliloquised Mrs. Thorpe to herself, adding aloud to her husband: I wish that Musgrove's sentence had not been respited. It will ruin Alec's chances of marrying _lrene, I am afraid.".

"I told her last night that in six months I shall be a bankrupt if she does not marry him. and he proposed to-day and was ac- cepted."

ZAM-BUK STOPS At I EPIDEMIC.

Ten in One Family Cured of Eczema. -- Doctors Astounded and Parents Crateful.

111.

---- - She looked towards the man, her husband, with eyes that spoke of fear and hate. "Do you know that we are the talk of ,the hotel? Do you know that one man said von looked as if you wanted to throw yourself. off the Gorner flrat-yesterday, in order to escape from me? Do you know that—" "Yes, I know I look wretched." "I'll leave Zermatt to-morrow. It's awful to be amongst a crowd of cheerful people with a.bride who looks like, a martyr. Why cannot you become interested in hll that is going on? There is plenty to see and talk about:" "Yes."

"Will you try to—" "I want to leave to-morrow," broke in Irene. "flon't let us co by train: let us (I.*e down to Visp. I think I shouta be happier away from this crowd of observant men and women, whose eyes watch me for ever." "All right, we'll drive down. The trains are certainly too crowded for comfort; and we will leave to-morrow."-

It was agreed, and the carriage was ordered. Ten o'clock on the morrow they drove oft at a good swinging pace from the hotel. It was a glorious day, sunshine and brigninege pervaded everywhere, and the glistening, snow-clad Matterhorn looked most lovely, sharply silhouetted against a bright blue sky. Through Taesch they drove, thon through Ronda, noting its vandyke chfdets, dotted about the ern'erald-green pastures.. ..

But Irene wore the Bailie look. of exquisite sadness that had irritated her husband for weeks.

"I can't stand it," he said to himself, fiercely, as he turned away from her. "If only—"

lie stopped thinking, stopped attempting to delineate the future.

The road had curved, and coming towards them, looking as if it were on the point of contesting the roadway, was an upward'. bound train. The horses started back, swerved aside, pranced, crushed against each other, each instant fording the carriage nearer and nearer to the edge of the pre- cipice. Now the train was nearly abreast, two minutes and it would be passed, and the danger would be passed too. "Get out! Let us get out! See, we she' be over the precipice in a second!" Alec St. John's voice rang out, laden with horror and terror.

Too late! Already the carriage was quivering at the edge. Now it was plunging down the precipice! The maddened steeds, who unwittingly had sought their tiwn terrible end, struggling and fighting to arrest their ghastly descent. Crash! Crash! Against impeding rocks the carriage was hurled as it sped down the mountain sida towards he fast-rushing river Viso—far, far below! It was an awful spectacle! Everyone in the train watched the terrible scene, shuddering and horror-stricken. Alec. clung successfully for a minute to the car- riage, then was hurled down the steep moun- tain side and at once lost to view. _ _

" Look ! The lady is sale!" cried an Eng lishman.

It was true! Irene hid just alighted on a ledge of projceting green sward, soft witb lovely ferns, and was now clinging to an over- hanging shrub. Beneath her, lashing itself into a mass of snow-white foam, rushed the angry waters of the Visp. Oh, heaven, to be saved from death at their cruel hands! •

The train had stopped. People were hurry- ing towards her—ropes, straps, all and every- thing that could enable others to come to her rescue were being fastened together. Now—oh, was it only ten minutes To her it seemed an eternity—someone was being lowered towards her.

" Are you hurt very much " he asked., ‘" No, I don't believe you are. We doctors are good judges of injured people at a glance. But you must be taken to the sum- mit at once.". _

"No ! I must be lowered to where my hus- band lies injured. I must speak to him quickly. I have his forgiveness to ask." Dr. Stewart cast a searching glance at the ashy-white and distressed face of the terrified girl, then shook his head. ' 'Don't tamper with your present state. You have received a most terrible shock. Although you do not appear to be absolutely injured, you are not in a condition to face any more mental strain." _ _

" I must speak to my husband, 1 must'" she stammered, in a choking voice. For agonies of regret filled her mind for having embittered his life during the last few weeks. Di. Stewart turned and looked down to where a poor battered mortal lay on the stones border- ing the river. "You shall have your way, then," he said, kindly. "I see that there is a narrow, zig-zag path leading down the mountain. If you will take my hand and let me help you we can manage the descent all right." In silence Irene complied. Dr. Stewart was a tall, gaunt, red-haired Scotchraan, about forty years of age, and with a confidence-inspiring manner.

Slowly and cautiously was that descent ac- complished. A quarter of an hour, and Irene stood at her husband's side.

He lived still. The . breath of life yet lingered in his crushed and suffering frame. Irene sank on her knees at his side.

"Oh, Alec, forgive me, for Ave me, for having niade you so unhappy! You cannot tell how bitterly I regret it," she said, be- tween her sobs. "If I could only live the time over again , I would be . a good wife." "It was only right and just that I shoUld be unhappy," he murmured. " I deserved it all, deserved to be a thousand times more wretched than you have made me. I have been cruel and wicked; no punishment was too 'great for me. Come nearer. Hold' my hand, whilst I confess my great sin to you." The next moment she tenderly clasped_ the poor limp band. " Ah, that is oomforting!" he whispered, then ceased to speak for a minute; only for a minute, for every second of time was most precious. • "Irene, you remember the murder of Robert Musgrove? vesou.

How could she forget it? ' " did it! He knew of my having forged a cheque years before, and he threatened to give information to the ,police. I knocked him down; then stabbeChim.' "And Anthony is innocent! Oh, Alec, how could you be—oh, no, I will not say a word of reproof." "If only I could live long enough to die with a clear conscience," he murmured, with a sob. Di. Stewart had moved away, arid now stood a short distance off, his face averted from the pathetic scene between the dying mEar and his young, beautiful wife. ".I will ask the .advice of the doctor who helped me to come to you," she said, rising. She told him all, and never has the history of a great wrong had been more briefly and pathetically told. "Can you help to clear away title terrible injustice ?" she said, looking up into his face most imnloringly. "Yes," he answered, with decision, "for I happen to be a J.P., And can take down your husband's evidence." .Saying this, Dr. Stewart walked quickly towards „Alec.

On a leaf torn from his note-book the con- fession of the crime was written ; then gently they raised the dying man to sign it. . - Strange was the scene, and most impres- sively harrowing. Within a few yards the fast-flowing Visp lashed itself into fury; and on both sides rose the steep, fir-clad moun- tains, snow-capped even on this August day. And outstretched on the grey stones lay Alec St. John, dying; glad that the ,weight of a great sin could haunt him no longer. The . end came quickly—after a few minutes.

...... Two days later, frems returned to England. Before a week passed Dr. Stewart placed Aloe's confession in the hands of the Home Secretary. Anthony Musgrore wax released—a thin, ::-r-ent, broken-hearted man. ' But the very first to welcome hita back to liberty wgs Irene. A month later tborr ,k placa a quiet mar- riage from the villas church, where once he I had been rector. • And from that day there began to steal into the ex-conyiet's face a look which rpoke of returning happiness. BRONCHITIS & BRONCHIAL COLDS-

October Dangers Averted by Peps.

Both old and young are liable to attacks tf bronchitis. Indeed, so common is this kind of bronchial cold That few are able to get through an Autumn without one or more attacks, .vvhile with a very large number of us the complaint has become chronic—largely, no doubt, because in the past the wrong method of treatment has been adapted. The discovery of Peps, however, opens the path of deliverance even to the chronic sufferer. _

Bronchitis is really inflammation of the delicate, membrane lining the tubes that carry pure air into.' the lungs. This membrane is extremely sensitive to chill or any sudden change of temperature. We notice this sensitiveness when breathing of cold night air is painful to us. A peculiar tickling and huski— ness in the throat, followed by intense nervous irrita— tion in the region of the breast-bone, may then be: noticed, while increased secretion of the fluid gireP rise to a choking sensation and a cleshe to " cough.: something up." • The rational method of treating bronchitis is by inhalation ; that is, breathing the medicine in shape of fumes from the mouth into the throat and chest and experience proves that inhalation of the balsamic 4 fumes emitted from Peps is ideal and offers the' auickest and only safe cure. Peps are a unique concentration of the particular natural, substances which Nature has furnished for • the cure of human throat and chest troubles, and their action embodies Nature's own way to the lungs. As the Peps tablet dissolves in the month, the curative fumes which it emits are inhaled with the breath. They bathe the air-passages and have the most soothing and healing influence. Liquid cough mixtures are merely swallowed into the stomach, and conse- quentlydo not reach the lungs at all. Peps are i unique n that, they go direct to the chest disease. Peps also contain no opium. chloral, or other of the harmful drugs to be found in cough mixtures and lozenges. Keep your box of Peps always handy—by your bedside for that troublesome night cough. A course of these splendid tablets will entirely cure bronchitis-4 and prevent a return of these attacks that have been getting worse year by year. Sold by all chemists Is. 11d. or 2s. Od. per box.

PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica.

EINIODIFI CATION IN PRINTING. It is very useful to the photographer to know that even with the same class of paper, very different prints may be made from a negative, without any " faking.':: For instance, with any given negative, and any given: printing paper, considerable difference may be made ill the" softness "or " hardness " of the print. If we - have an exceedingly' dense, bard negative, and are printing, say, on P.0.P., it may take a couple of days in diffused light. and the print will be very hard and contrasty. The brilliant negative, printed on the earn paper, in direct trilliant sunshine, may take half air hour or forty minutes, and the result will be relatively harmonious. THE EFFECT OF THE PRINTING LIGHT.' The example mentioned in the above paragraph iven the general principle which is to be followed. iVith a , dense, harsh negative, print in strong light ; with is' soft, flat negative print in weak light. This is the,4 secret of the great success of the "printing under grees.': glass" which was popular some years ago. The same' effect can be obtained by printing in very weak diffused light,. Not only with daylight -and print-out rapers, but also with artificial light and development papers, the same general principle holds good. In working bromide, for instance, if it is desired to aware softness from a hard negative, it is well to print quite close to a lamp giving a strong light. While' with the same paper, if one wishes to get a relatively plucky print from a fairly soft flat negative, the frame should be- removed two of three or more feet from the light. ' Of course, in the latter case, the time of exposure will be very considerably increased, for the power of the light diminishes inversely as the square of the distance. Thor, if a given negative requires one second at one foot from the lamp, it will require sixteen seconds at four feet frdm the same light. Of course, if the negative printed at one foot is a very dense one, and that print( 1 at four is thin, the exposure for the latter will be. sixteen times, but perhaps from eight to Len times what Is required for the former. THE INFLUENCE OF THE PAPER. Different printing papers bare very different scales of gradation so that with a given negative the best possiblw print on one payer may he hard while the best possible , print en another paper may be soft and fiat. It is for this reason that the success of tl e photographers deperde very largely upon the way in which they contrA their exposure and their development, to give them negatives capable of producing the prints they want on the - particular paper they prefer to use. It is not necessary to mention here all the differences in gradation between the various papers, but for the current winter time it is perhaps well to remind readers, with regard to bromide, that, generally speaking, rapid papers tend to give soft results, while slow papers tend to give harsh and more brilliant prints.

To duplex paraffin burner with clear glass chimney. 120 sec. at Gin, To al6 c.p. incandescent electric lamp, 00sec. at 61n.

Wherefore, the following outline. table (given for Wellington booklets, just issued) maY be useful : EXPOSURES AT A DISTANCE OF H INCHES. To en ordinary (" batswing ") gas burner, dsrc. There so- now a couple of examples of work made noon this plate to 1:e seen at tho exhibition of The Royal Photo- graphic Society, and there is some probability of its coming on to the-market. OUR PATRON, HER MAJESTY THE QUEEN. All photographers know that Queen Alexandra is n°t. only a patron of photogrtpl err, and of the R.P.S. kilt also an enthusiastic photographer herself. Therefore I sin sure,that they will all support the effort made the Queen and by ‘• The Daily Telegraph" to raise: funds for charitable purposes by tie publication a i"Photograms from my Camera ; Queen Alexancirs'S' Christmas Gift-hook"; 130 pagss, consisting of repro- ductions of the Queen's pictures, with notes written bY herself, may be obtained for 2s. 6d., and photogrnphero will doubtless use numbers of these gift books amongst their Christmas presents. There is no Parliamentary WORK FOR THE disagreement as to the WORKLESS. necessity of doing something to help the unemployed. The only differences of opinion arise out of con- siderations of method. That the proposals of the Government are not to be opposed on Parlia- mentary grounds was immediately made apparent by Mr. BALFOUR on the conclusion of Mr. Asourra'a speech on Wednesday. The Leader of the Opposition thanked the PRIME MINISTER for his "extraordinarily clear statement," and was only anxious to know when a date can be given for its discussion. The case was the same with Mr. ARTHUR HENDERSON, the leader of the Labour party. Neither Mr. BALFOUR nor Mr. HENDERSON was willing to commit himself on the moment to any criticism of the Ministerial scheme, but it may be taken for granted that this present hesitation does not mean future hostility. Such differences as will ultimately manifest them- selves may be confidently expected to deal only with details. The Labour party will contend that the Government proposals do not go far enough. On the Conservative benches it may be maintained that there should have been some variation of methods. These are propositions that the Govern- ment will not dispute. Mr. ASQUITH does not pretend that he is dealing with the unemployment question in a definite manner. He proposes a temporary alleviation of admitted evils, and he will certainly not refuse to listen to suggestions as to how the measure of relief proposed by the Government may be more effectively applied. Before the present Parliament comes to an end, the PRIME MINISTER observed, it is hoped, and it is intended, that a real and effective blow should ho struck at the permanent causes of unemploy- ment. The proposals now laid before the House and the country are intended to meet a special emergency. They are destined, in fact, for the purpose of tiding us over the coming winter. The Government and the local authorities, he was able to show, have done i great deal more than they have received credit for. The expenditure, taking the whole country through, has really been very large, but Mr. Asourrtit does not contend that it has been enough. Some local authorities have proved themselves to be indisposed to do anything, and for that reasod, among others, he sets aside the suggestion that the Government should authorise the levying of an unemployment rate. However, he made a'statement which goes much further than anything that has been said by any other PRIME MINISTER on this subject. The unemployed, he remarked,have a right to demand something more than sympathy, and he added that it is the duty of Parliament to give them more. This is the deliberate acceptance of a grave responsibility. Unemployment is, from this moment, made a national affair. The Government proposals will be considered in some quarters to be of a poor and limited character. From other directions they will be severely criticised as a departure from severe economies. But there is no proposal against which romething cannot be said from opposite points of view. What is abundantly clear for the present is that the plane decided upon as a tempo- rary alleviation of public suffering will cost a great deal of money, and that they are absolutely practical. Local authorities are to be encouraged to give work to the unemployed instead of con- tracts for labour; but as they always lose money in such cases, the difference of cost varying from 5 to 40 per cent., the Government is to make up the difference of cost between contract and unemployed work. The idea is, of course, to encourage contem- plated undertakings that are at present being delayed either for want of means or because they are not pressing. At Christmas the Post Office will take on 8,000 extra hands at a wage of 253. per week preference being given to married men. There will be additional Post Office employment in the larger towns. The Army Special Reserve is pre- pared to spend £200,000 in taking on 24,000 extra men, and the Navy will provide work, chiefly on repairs, to the extent of £63,500. It will also enter at once on additional shipbuilding work. Further, it is proposed to take the large step of permitting assistance to be given to persons who have received poor law relief withiu the preceding twelve months and to remove the disqualifintions of those who have been assisted during the last two years. The plan is fairly comprehensive, at least, ankthough it is necessarily open to criticism it seems likely to go through Parliament without much change of form.

The cold snap provides the THE COMING OF latest eccentricity in the FROST. weather in a year whose summer and autumn have been full of quaint surprises. Frost on the Con tinent, with the grape harvesters knee deep in snow, has this element of appropriateness, that it follows a misplaced summer which had somehow got posponed to September. The element of

unexpectedness has a perennial charm ; and twenty-five degrees of frost in the third week of October has, at any rate, that virtue to recommend it. With the temperature in London but a few degrees above freezing-point, and the cold area reaching to Hungary and 'Moldavia, we are faced with a phenomenon which registers the passing of the seasons. South Germany, with twenty-five degrees of frost, has reached a record in cold for October, while the 400 tons of water per acre which fell in the district of Portland and Weymouth were, in their way, unprecedented. After all,nobody wants an Indian summer to extend icto November, and we are within a week of the end of October. The colder weather is the more wholesome and seasonable,and the advent of conditions which are bracing and fresh is upon every ground to be welcomed.

We would direct the special

THE attention of our readers to MUSICAL SOCIETY. the letter, published in our correspondence columns, from Mr. J. P. Reynolds, hon. treasurer of the Warrington Musical Society, in which he appeals for increased public support for the work of that estimable organization. For seventy-five years I the Musical Society has been doing splendid service in the cause of musical culture, but it has not met with the practical encouragement which it undoubtedly deserves. It is not to the credit of the town that the society should be handicapped by straitened resources, and it behoves lovers of music to zee that the reproach is removed. Last season there was a notable quickening of interestl in the concerts on the part of the general public, but even under these improved conditions the loss amounted to £45. What the society most needs at the present time is a greatly augurnented list of subscribers. The programme for the coming session is a most attractive one and when one considers the high class work in hand and the careful study and heavy expense involved, it ought surely not to be too much to expect those who are in a position to patron'z a those concerts to do so ungrudgingly. No exception can be taken to the terms of subscription, which are very moderate indeed, and the slightest reflection should convince anyone of the justice of the Society's claim to increased help from the townspeople. We hope Mr. Reynolds' timely appeal will not be in vain. Warrington has made a great advance in the musical world of late years, and onr Musical Society is entitled to no small share of the credit for this and for the happy revival which has been witnessed in all matters musical. Its educational work is bearing good fruit, and it would be a gratifying thing if Warrington would show its appreciation by rally- ing to the support of so worthy an organization as the Musical Society in no half-hearted way. An article from the Mon- WHEN TEE ANTI- treal correspondent of the DUMPER LIKES "Liverpool Post and Mer- D unmet. cury " published last Satur- day, makes a curious revelation concerning the well-known local firm of Pearson and Knowles, Ltd. The writer describes the operations of the Dominion Iron and Steel Company of Canada, which has been highly- favoured by Government bounties and special protective legislation, and has in consequence done more dumping than any other iron and steel manufacturing undertaking in the Dominion of Canada." Pig-iron has been largely exported to— or dumped in—England, and the writer makes the assertion that some consignments went to the Pearson and Knowles Company, and the soft impeachment has not been denied. The "Liver- pool Post" editorially chaffs the local company about taking advantage of this cheap raw material and presumes, not unnaturally, that "Canadian bounties have been helping the Warrington firm to earn its ill per cent. dividend and to keep its men employed." The humour of the situation is increased when we recall that at the annual meeting of the Pearson and Knowles Com- pany on September :29,th last, Mr. W. H. Blackly said that " all the directors were fiscal reformers, not as politicians, but as men ..of business." It is obvious that Mr. Bleckly never told the truth with more literal exactitude. As Tariff Reformers the directors protest strongly against the iniquitous foreign "dumper," but when he dumps raw material useful to themselves then they recollect that business must come first and Tariff Reform last. It is a charming illustra- tion of the way Tariff Reform hits back. As if to accentuate the irony of the position, the Dominion Iron Company has secured the order for 9,000 tons of steel rails for the Indian railways. It was one of Mr. W. H. Bleckly's sorest points that the Indian market was being lost by us to the foreigner owing to "his unfair advantages." Apparently the foreigner can present us with the cheap raw material and still beat us in the market. How is Tariff Reform going to remedy this difficulty?

The drink interest is playing

. - THE "TRADE" so prominent a part in the AND THE VOTER, elections locally that it i very necessary that rate- payers should look matters squarely in the face. We have already six members of the Council who are directly interested in either the manufacture or the sale of drink, and one of them holds the important position of deputy-chairman of the Watch Committee—the Committee which has most to do with the administration of the licensing laws in the borough. Oae sixth of the whole Council is Eurely more than a fair proportion of representation for any one Trade to possess. If there were six butchers, or bakers, or tailors on the Council nearly everyone would be exclaiming at the absurdity and rank injustice of it, but the Trade—the dangerous Trade—can apparently secure an unlimited number of representatives on local governing bodies, and no one complains except temperance people, and they run the risk of being dubbed faddists and fanatics in consequence. The question is not a temperance one solely. It concerns the welfare of the whole community, and it is time people showed some alarm at the ten- dency of affairs. The Trade is not satisfied with six representatives out of thirty-six, and a seventh is boldly seeking support in Orford Ward. We sincerely hope he will not get it. The voters, if they exercise the franchise like true citizens, will set their faces sternly against the return of any Trade nominees to the Town Council. They will secure two excellent and trustworthy representa- tives if they vote for Councillor Ball and Mr. P. Peacock.

The Warrington Citizens' Temperance League has done excellent work in its brief existence in the direction of educating the people in the moral issues of sobriety and clean living. There is plenty of work for such an organisatiori, and we are glad to note that the League is coming forward with pertinent questions for the candidates now seeking the suffrages of the electors. It must, in the future,be made as bard for a publican to enter the Warrington Town Council as it is for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle. Temperance effort—we do not mean teetotal—had long lacked co-ordination in Warrington, and we have hopes that this non-political and unsectarian league of citizens will accomplish a great deal in the direc- tion of ridding the town of the incubus of "Trade" dominance which, like an" old man of the sea" exerts a strangling hold on the throat of civic progress.

Lord Lamington, as the IMPROVING THR author of R bill for the Punic-HousE. Extension of Facilities in

- _ Public-Houses introduced in the House of Lords before the adjournment, writes an interesting article in the " National Review." We are concerned with his Lordship's criticism of the Government's Licensing Bill,partly because it adds nothing new to the controversy on that measure, and partly because we think it is not really material to. his case. The object of Lard Lamington's Bill is tot the extension of the facilities for drinking. Whatever criticism may be offered to his scheme, he must be given credit for desiring to improve public-houses by rendering them less mere drinking saloons and mote public resorts at which decent people may be able to obtain with comparative ease other forms of refreshment than intoxicating liquors, at which the arbitrary discomfort of the bar may be

exchanged for the more civilised arrangement of small tables, at which games, newspapers, music, and other forms of recreation may be provided. The idea is not new. Other reformers have aimed at a change of this discription ; and, like Lord Lamington, they have pointed to the example of continental countries like Franco and Germany, where the caféi afford the facilities he desires, and where insobriety in the forms we know it is practically non-existent. There is some reason in the contention that if public-houses are to be permitted to exist—and prohibition is obviously a ' long way off yet—they should be reformed, and the lines which are suggested in this 811 l are clearly not such as would be likely to work a tithe of the evil which will be wrought by the continu- ance of the system of " drinking dens" which at present exists. At any rate, the Bill is worthy of consideration.

Tne remarkable statistics UNEMPLOYMENT which D. Macnamara IN PROTECT/DNIST voted at a public meeting COUNTaIRS. at Peckham in regard to unemployment in ''Protec- tionist countries have been confirmed by the report of the French Government Dopartment of Lsbour. The presont proportion of unemployment in France is 2 per cent, above the normal. This is traced to over-production in the iron and steel trade, following on tire boom caused by the Russo-Japanese War. The American financial crisis has contributed to the depression by producing a great decline in the demand for French jewellery. At the same time, the French Daparttnent of Labour states that the minimum unemployment in France is higher than the minimum in Great Britain, "owing to the organization of British labour." Trade depression is felt everywhere, but a Protectionist Government like France admits that it is felt less keenly in Great Britain though there is less agitation about unemployment in France, because the evil is commoner there and creates less surprise. Speak- ing in S. Helens on Sunday, De. S idekum, a member of the German R a oichsta, said there had not been so many unemployed in his own country for many years as there were this year. He declared emphatically that "Protection was no remedy, and it could not be said that the propor tion of misery was greater in the unprotected country than in the protected country." We wonder how much longer we shall hear it said with parrot-like reiteration that " Tariff reform means work for all"

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

November Ist will be the 60th anniversary of the opening of the Warrington Museum and Library. Local Liberals are eagerly looking forward to the visit to Warrington on November 28th of Mr. Birrell, who will address a great meeting in the Parr Hall. The Borough Member will also be present. * * *

Congratulations and best wishes to the Rev. John Yonge, the father of Nonconformity in Warrington, who attained his 70th birthday yesterday. As the years roll on,the circle of his friends and well-wishers ever widens !

* * * Stockton Heath people have been waiting upon the Tramways Committee to secure a reduction of the fare to Stockton Heath to Id., or a division of the route into halfpenny stages. The Committee has declined to accede to either of these requests. It is getting high time they made concessions, and some form of contract ticket on the busier routes would be a boon.

* * * Mrs. Winston Churchill has very quickly given proof that shs is prepared to discharge the public duties that fall to the lot of the wife of a Cabinet Minister. On Tuesday afternoon she opened the bazaar at the Horticultural Hall in aid of the work of the Browning Settlement. She did so in a charming little speech, excellently delivered, and clataining just the right sentiment for the occasion. Mrs. Churchill will henceforth have to be counted among the lady orators of the Liberal party.* The Victoria Park Bowling Green has demonstrated its recreative usefulness by being crowded with bowlers on all possible occasions during the summer. The receipts for the four years it has been in use have been as follows :-1905, £ll 4s. id.: 1906, £54 10s. 21. ; 1907, £45 us. 6d. ; 1908, £53 Bs. 6d. We hope a determined effort will be made by the Town Hall and Parks Committee this winter, to com- plete the scheme for three additional greens in time for next season. They are already six months be hindhand.

* * * It seems to be an accepted fact that Alderman Forshaw is to be the next Mayor of Warrington. He was selected by a large majority of the members at a caucus meeting of the Council on Monday, though there was some division of opinion on the Conservative side. The peculiar method of selection adopted at the last Council meeting has worked badly, as we antici- pated, and considerable friction has resulted. How- ever, Alderman Forshaw should make a worthy successor to Alderman Smethurst, and if he does not get unanimous support on November 9th, it is hardly likely to be the fault of his political opponents.

Although Mr. John BiTrns is the first Labour Member of Parliament who has gone or could go as Cabinet Minister to visit the King, he is by no means the first Labour Member who has been entertained at Sandringham. Mr. Joseph Arch was genially treated as "my member" by the then Prince of Wales, and was on several occasions an honoured guest at Sand- ringham, of which he delighted to tell pleasant stories. Mr. Henry Broadhurst was also there for a week-end, and he has recorded his memories of the occasion in his own account of his own life. Mr. Broadhurst commented on the solidity of the chairs in the village ale-house. "I dare say some of our politicians wish their seats were as safe," said the Prince with a * *

Mrs. John Crosfield has; way of doing good by stealth. Just lately she has been evidencing her characteristic kindliness by entertaining the porters and platform men from the Bank Quay and Central Stations at Bryn Eithin, Colwyn Bay. It can hardly be said of the railway porter that he gets more kicks than halfpence, but he certainly performs many services for the general public without getting due reward. People have oftener a growl than a gracious smile for " Mr. Porter," who is quite inured to the task of suffering for the sins of his particular railway company. Mrs. Crosfield's forethought and sumptuous hospitality have been greatly appreciated by the men of corduroy and buttons.

* * * It is seldom that so " broad " a platform is seen at a public meeting as the one which faced the Parr Hall audience on Monday evening, when the Warrington Citizens' Temperance League held its second great demonstration in support of the Licensing Bill now passing through the House of Commons. The Dean of Carlisle (a veteran temperance stalwart) occupied the chair and was supported by. other distinguished Churchmen, a Labour M.P., ministers and laymen of almost every Nonconformist denomination, Liberals, Conservatives and Socialists. The speaking was as ' interesting and varied as the platform, and the valu- able points of the Bill have seldom been better expounded. The most remarkable feature was the total absence of beneficed clergymen representing the ancient parish of Warrington. Though their Bishops have given an unmistakable lead on the question at issue to-day, the clergy of Warrington have been conspicuously absent from the Temperance platform. It seems pretty obvious that the shadow of the brewery dwarfs the shadow of the church spire. * Vancouver is a long way off. This may account to some extent for the following item of news which appeared in the "Vancouver World" the other day : London, September 21.—Maddened by suffering, over a million and a half of Britain's unemployed have become little better than savages during the last few days, and the authorities of the United Kingdom are at a loss for means with which to cope with the situation, which daily becomes more menacing. Riots occur daily in all the large cities. . . Services were broken up yesterday in cyclones of disorder at the Cathedrals at Canter- bury, Mancliester,Norwich and Glasgow by denuncia- tions of the starving. . . . Admitting that they are unable to cope with the situation, the police at dozens of manufacturing centres have called for military assistance to put down the riots which have already occurred. . . . All over the country bodies of soldiers are being stationed at strategic points to be ready for outbreaks. Bismarck once said that nothing lied like a telegrams and time gives the saying proof.

More than one amateur gardener who has passed Ai up or down Market-street, Manchester, during the last week or two and seen the profusion of copper beech branches that adorn the front of one of the flower-shops there must have felt much inclined to quarrel with his own copper beeches at home. For never was there copper beech of so glorious a red as this one. It so took my eye (writes "B " in the "Manchester Guardian ") that I went into the shop and asked what kind of copper beech it was. I learnt to my astonishment that the colour was hardly all the tree's own. "Copper beech leaves," one of the assistants explained, "are in great demand now for decorative purposes, and we have a preparation that not only preserves the leaves but improves their colour. People like them a rich red, you see," The flower-dyeing industry is no new one, as our hawkers green carnations show, but tree dyeing is surely a ne- departure.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[FRO3I OUR LONDON CORRESPONDENT.] The complaint is often heard that" there is nothing in the newspapers this morning." At the present moment, however, what with a European situation that shifts almost from day to day, and a House of Commons strenuously grappling with the task of legislation, those who take a serious interest in public affairs have more reason to complain that there is so much happening that they cannot beep pace with the movement. On Friday, October 16 th, Mr. Asquith announced an important modification in the Licensing Bill, not, of course, with the hope of placating those who are hostile to the principles of the Bill, but with the object, as he stated, of satisfying even the most far-fetched scruples of anybody who thinks that injustice might in particular cases have been done by it." Then, Mr. Harcourt, on behalf of the Government, has moved amendments bringing off-licences within the scope of the measure, and dealing with local option. The Children's Bill has passed its third reading, and the House has decided that the London and Dis- trict Electric Supply Bill shall go into Committee. Mr. Asquith's statement as to Unemployment was, perhaps, the most important event of an eventful week, and the necessary reconstruction, consequent upon the retirement from public life of Lord Tweedmouth and Lord Ripon, has been completed by the transference of Mr. McKinnon Woods from the Board of Education to the Undar-Secretaryship for Foreign Affairs, and the appointment of Mr. Charles Trevelyan to the Board of Education.

TRIBUTE TO A VETERAN STATESXIAN. There is not a single psrson in the political world who, irrespective of party, fails to experience deep regret that the burden of years, aggravated by 3 domestic bereavement, has compelled Lord Ripon to withdraw from the responsibilities of office. The loss of his calm wisdom and of his sweet and gracious personality will be keenly felt by his colleagues. An idealist of a lofty type, Lord Ripon was a living reminder of high traditions in a period of gloom, when idealism was scouted and derided. HappilYr his public career has not been closed without the gratification of seeing the principles that he cherished with so much devotion revived with a fresh access of energy. A statesman of singular disinterestedness and charm, he was also —it must not be overlooked the best-beloved of all Indian Viceroys among the native peoples of the British " Raj."

NEW MINISTERS. The appointment of Mr. NcKinnon Wood to the Foreign Office will give Sir Edward Grey a needed relief relief in these days of pressure in the task of answering questions in the House. At the same times Lord Fitzmaurice will act as spokesman for the Foreign Office in the House of Lords, where, as nLorr a member of the Cabinet, he will be able to speak with more, not less, authority. Mr. McKinnon Wood had given exceptional proof of ability in municipal affairs, proof that was fully confirmed by his short term the Board of Education. Mr. Trevelyan brings to lue new office not only an honoured name, but the reputation of being one of the most brilliant of a brilliant galaxy of rising young Liberals.

MR. ASQUITH'S CONCESSION. The concession which Mr. Asquith foreshadowed on Friday week was inspired by no hope of " buying; off " the hostility of the root-and-branch opponents oT the Bill." The time-limit laid down in the Bill Noe based on the principle that sufficient time should.he given for a prudent trader to make adequate provision to recoup himself for that part of the property which is to be attributed to the monopoly value. The Opposition admitted, on Mr. Asquith's challenge, that hey would be placated by no extension of the time limit. It is, therefore, not to them, but to over- anxious supporters of the principles of the Bill, syhe fear that there may be some cases of hardship arising from it with a fourteen-year time-limit, that Mr- Asquith has addressed himself. His proposal is that 'Compensation should cease after fourteen years, and that at the end of that time every licence shall be held at the unfettered discretion of those who represent the community. But in order to avoid even the shadow of harshness it is now suggested that a further period —probably seven years—should be granted, durini which the State will not resume the possession or monopoly value. OFF-LICENCES AND MONOPOLY VALUE. It is an undoubted strengthening of the Bill that the monopoly value of "off " licences will, under the amendment moved by Mr. Harcourt, be secured after the reduction period. It is true that at the present time" off "licences have no monopoly value, but 30 is quite possible that after the period of reduction 3 monopoly value may grow up. Words have accordiosl9 been added to the Bill to secure that if any monop9ll value in" off " licenses should come into operatio? between now and the end of the reduction period, It would be secured to the State. The-criticism, how- ever mistaken or unfounded, is thus obviated tin!! the Bill was favouring " off " at the expense of" on licences ; or, in crude terms, sacrificing the publicae for the benefit of the grocer. LOCAL OPTION. After the reduction period, local option will extrucl to new and old on-licences, which after that period will all become technically new licences, and a tw°' thirds majority of those voting will be necessary f°r the carrying of the resolution. These provisions are to be extended "in such manner as Parliament FnaY determine," so asito authorise a resolution " the number of licences to those existing at the tirne,or reducing that number." A good deal of anxiety wee felt about this legacy of an instruction, as it were, the Parliament of fourteen years hence. Sir Thonre Whittaker urged that nothing should be done which might have a limiting effect. The Government, how- ever, wished to guard against the slightest possibility- of complaint on the part of "the trade " that,, Parliament had not given them sufficient notico. er what was likely to happen. The amendment 0'439 notice to " the trade " that there are, at any rate, twe options to which they must look forward. But it does` not in any way prevent a future Parliament floe' devising others.

THE DISASTER OF 1.895. Keir Hardie had something interesting to 531 in regard to the idea that the Liberal Party had beell over-thrown owing to the Temperance Bill of 189J' As one of the victims of that time, he claimed to 'be able to speak with some authority. It was not, 136' contended, the introduction of the Bill, but the failure to put it through, that caused the disaster. Tb° Bill raised the fighting instincts of the liquor interest' The failure to proceed with it opposed only a lakevval Temperance party to a liquor interest determined 0„ fully alive to the issue they had at stake. It it: point of view which has a distinct bearing upon th present crisis.

CHILDREN AND THE STATE. The Government has been most scrupulous not 0 imperil the safe passage of the Children's Bill 11 raising unnecessary points of controversy, or by oce,r: loading it. And Mr. Akers-Douglas declared in tP: debate on the final stage, that the Bill is in no sens,°.; party one. At the same time common sense and Vl,lO play demand some recognition of the fact that too Liberal Government is taking a new departure of tYai most important kind in pressing on with 0%. reforms that do not lend themselves to party tactic And some members of the Opposition showed their selves very ready to raise objections. Why cre.„Ai; new offences? Why invade family life? "be weaken parental responsibility? It ought to,g, emphasised on every possible occasion that the 91, not only aims at protecting child life by penal eria: meats, but at raising the general standard of morall and responsibility in regard to child-neglect. Ingtet, of weakening parental responsibility it strengtheno for when the law declares various forma of to be not only culpable but punishable, the eyes great masa of ignorant populatioa will be ' wrongs which carelessness; has condoned.

On Tuesday evening a public meeting in support of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts was held in the Parr Hall. There was a large attendance, the principal feature of the pro- ceedings being a lecture by the Rev. Canon Westcott, who has devoted nearly twenty years of his life to missionary work at Cawnpore. The Ven. Archdeacon Spooner, who presided,said that no country was really alive and expanding which did not do something in the matter of colonization, and it was so with regard to the Christian Church. Unless she was lengthening her cords and strengthening her stakes it was per- fectly clear that there was no vitality,and with regard tO that branch of the Church Catholic to which they belonged, especially as centred in their own country, unless she was sending forth the representatives of ' her teaching and the pioneers of the Gospel in every direction, it was perfectly clear that she was not expending. (Applause) /le wished all Nesent that night to pray that their hearts might be enlarged, their sympathies extended, and that they might, so to speak, reach outward and onward, and try to do all they possibly could to advance the cause of Christ in other lands, and by cl,ning so they would reap a benefit in their own ncines. (Applause.) The lecture by Canon Westcott was illustrated with a large number of beautiful views which he has been asked to exhibit before the Royal §ociety, and proved highly interesting and instructive. On the proposition of the Mayor, seconded by the Rector, a vote of thanks was accorded to the chair man, lecturer and organist (Mr. B. Randall). A collection was taken and realized £l3 103. Gd. Ober Ammergau Play. The London Animated Picture Company, brought their fortnight's visit to the Parr Hall to a close on Sunday evening by giving a sacred concert. The chief feature was a fine series of views of the Ober Ammergan Passion Play depicting the childhood and public life of Christ and His Passion and death. Appropriate.songs were well rendered by Hiss Helena Sleuth. _ _

----- Sequel to a Football Match. Patrick Patten, of Cockhedge-lane, Warrington, and a Wigan youth named Joseph Lattice, School-street, were charged at the Warrington Police Court on Monday with fighting in II orsemarket-street on Saturday night. Au argument arose about the foot- ball match between Warrington and Wigan, which culminated ix a free fight. A fine of ss. each was imposed. _ _

Wycliffe Young People's Society. The winter session of this society was inaugurated on Monday evening when a social was held in the Memorial Hall. The Rev. John Young presided. Songs were rendered by the Misses Alderton and Griffiths, Messrs. B. Hibbert and H. Hawthorn, and recitations by the Misses Corney and Jordan. Between 40 and 50 members attended, and a very pleasant evening was spent.

Band of Hope Union. The third of the series of lectures to Band of Hope and temperance workers on the scientific aspects of temperance was delivered in Bold-street Church Parlour on Tuesday evening by Mr. J. Jakeman, of Liverpool. In the absence of Councillor Ball, the chair was occupied by Ur. Clatwortly. The subject of the lecture was " How alcohol affects the heat of the body " and it was dealt with in a very interesting and instructive manner. A vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman on the proposition of Mr. Muir seconded by Mr. Dale.

Alleged Poaching. At the Warrington Occasional Court, on Monday, John Skelland, Bostock-street, was charged with being in possession of a quantity of rabbits unlaw- fully obtained. Early in the morning of September 29th, the prisoner, along with three other men, who have been dealt with by the magistrates was seen by Police-constable Preston coming along the railway embankment at Acton Grange. In his possession were found nets and pegs and fifty-nine rabbits. Prisoner was remanded to the Daresbury Petty Sessions. _ _

Crippled Girls' Floral Exhibition. We would remind our readers that a six days' grand floral exhibition and sale of beautiful artificial flowers made by blind and crippled girls which has been organized by the Watercress and Flower Girls' Christian Mission will commence at the Parr Rail on Monday next. The exhibition will be opened at three o'clock by the Mayoress, and the Mayor (Iderman Smethurst) will preside. A contingent of the afflicted girls will be at work each afternoon and evening in making various kinds of flowers. No paper flowers are made. The exhibition will be open each following day from 11 a.m. to 9.30 p.m. There will be no charge for admission. Refreshments will be provided at moderate charges, and there will be music each afternoon and evening. We hope the exhibition will be well supported by the townspeople for the work of the institution is worthy of every commendation.

PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BIST BOOTS IN LANCASTIIRE.—Advt. ' Warrington Shop Thieves. At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, Joseph Abrahams, Back Haydock-street, was charged with stealing a pair of boots value is. 11d, from outside the shop of Alfred Taylor, Winwick-street, and further, with stealing a pair of trousers, value 2s. Ild, from the shop of Messrs. Jackson, pawnbrokers.—The Chief Constable said that a boy saw prisoner cut the string which fastened the boots to the window ledge, and walk away with them. Information was given to the police, and he was arrested early on Sunday morning. With reference to the second charge the assistant in the pawnshop saw prisoner take the trousers. After a short chase he was captured, but owing to the shop being full of customers prisoner was allowed to go, but he was rearrested later.—The magistrates sent prisoner to gaol for three months. Penny Popular Concerts. The popular concert at Bold-street school on Saturday evening attracted another crowded audience, and the programme proved thoroughly acceptable. The committee spare no effort to please their patrons and they are deserving of much credit for the high- class entertainments they present. Master Robert Hamer, of Accrington, who is described as "the finest boy singer in the North of England, is a vocalist of much promise, and he was repeatedly encored for his singing. Miss Carrie Hastings, the well-known local vocalist, also treated the audience to several songs. Miss Lilian Manssuer, of Liverpool, and Mr. H. Plumpton also contributed to an admirable programme. Mr. Alfred Waterworth was the humorist, and he kept the audience in rare good humour.

Stiff Sentence on a Burglar. At the Chester Assizes on Tuesday, William Peel (SO), tailor, pleaded guilty to two charges of burglary and one of housebreaking.—The first two offences were at the houses of the Rev. L. J. T. Daman, of Walton, and Mr. John Lever Tillotson, Higher Bebington, and the last at the residence of Ur. George Norman, Stockton Heath.—Mr. Harrison, barrister (Manchester), explained that the offences were com- mitted in January, but the prisoner had been in the Warrington Infirmary since. When Peel was in Mr. Norman's honed he was disturbed, and jumped from a first-floor window. He was picked up in a collapsed state, having severe wounds from an open knife which he had in his pocket.— Inspector Lees said Peel was a confirmed criminal, having eighteen convictions, which amounted to 33 years.—Mr. Justice Lawrence observed that the prisoner appeared to have been engaged in a life of crime since 1881, and had had sentences of ten, three, and four years. He was an instance which proved the views put forward by a recent report on the effect of short sentences, namely, that although they were useful in many cases, they did not operate in others, particularly burglary. Burglaries were increasing, and the crime apparently was made a profession. Prisoner would have to go to penal servitude for seven years.

Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary. The Hon. Secretary to the Workingmen's Com- mittee begs to acknowledge, with thanks, the sum of 4s. ld., received from Hr. W. Patrick, Hon. Secretary Warrington Works Bowling Association, being proportion of proceeds of charity match played August 22nd, DOS. Fire at Bank Quay Station. A fire broke out on Saturday night in a small shed situated a short distance beyond the platform at Bank Quay Station, and containing a quantity of oil, &c, used for illuminating purposes. The Fire Brigade were telephoned for, and they quickly arrived on the scene under the direction of the Superintendent and Deputy-Superintendent, but their services were not required, as the Company's fire brigade found them- selves able to cope with the outbreak. The damage is slight.

PUBLIC BENEFIT UNDER VTR CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—AdVt. Market Stall Theft. William Harry Forrest, a school boy, of 16 Gilbert- street, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Monday, with stealing two shirts, the property of Harry Percival, from a market stall on Sunday, 18th October.—lt appears that at 11.20 on Sunday morning the defendant was seen by a police officer at the prosecutor's stall, which is slightly broken, abstract- ing the clothing by means of a fork, which enabled him to hook them out. When defendant saw the officer he ran away and hid underneath a stall, but was caught. When questioned at the Police Station he admitted having stolen a shirt the previous Sunday. —The by in answer to the charge said that his mother had told him to steal the articles. She gave him the fork to hook them out and she waited outside the market for him.—The Chief Constable stated that the mother had absconded.—The boy after a severe reprimand from the Mayor, was ordered to receive six strokes with the birch rod.

" Muria England." The Royal Court Theatre was crowded in every part on Saturday evening when the operatic members of the Warrington Musical Society gave their conclud- ing performance of "Morrie England." It was an evening of triumphs. Everything went with a delightful swing and the principals were constrained to respond to encores time after time. At the close there was a remarkably enthusiastic demonstration. The curtain was rung up and as the leading characters passed across the stage they were the recipients of bouquets and numerous other presents from their admirers. In response to loud and repeated calls Mr. Frank IL Crossley, the musical director and con- ductor, Mr. Foden Williams, the coach and stage manager, who have worked so indefatigably for the success of the production, came forward and were applauded to the echo. The performers then joined in singing " Auld Lang Syne, ' thus bringing to a close a week which will long be recalled with pleasure by all concerned. Special mention should be made of the valuable services of Mr. Fred J. Reece, the popular acting manager of the Royal Court Theatre, to whose willing co-operation it was largely due that the arrangements worked so smoothly.

Shop-lifting by Warrington Women. - At Staffordshire Quarter Sessions, on Tuesday, Annie Miller (43), married, and her daughters,Miriam (21), and Annie (22), servants who gave an address in Chorlton-street, off Forest-street, Latchford, War- rington, were charged with haying, at Longton, on September 16th, stolen two watches from the shop of Edith Andrews, jeweller, Stafford-street, and a gold wedding ring from the shop of William Thorpe Simmonds, jeweller,Market-terrace. Mr. B. C. Brough for the prosecution stated that the women called at the two shops ostensibly to buy a wedding ring, but they made no purchase. When they had left Miss Andrew's shop two watches were missed off the counter and Mr.Simmouds missed a wedding ring from his shop. Miriam took the responsibility for the thefts upon her shoulders, but the other prisoners denied the charges. Mrs. Miller said that Miriam told her, as they were riding home in the tram, that she had taken two watches,and she then urged Miriam to take them back to the shop.—On the charge of stealing the watches the mother and Annie were found not guilty, bat they were convicted of stealing the ring.—Detective- Sergeant Meacock stated that the elder and younger prisoners had been in trouble at Warrington.—The Assistant Chairman (sir Reginald Hardy) said the mother, who should have kaown better, would have to go to gaol for nine months, and the two daughters for sa months. PUBL/0 BENKTIT.—IINDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. A Deserving Institution. . .

Among the deserving of the many philanthropic institutions of our great Metropolis must be numbered Mr. John A. Groom's Industrial Training Home for Afflicted, Blind and Crippled Girls in connection with , the Watercress and Flower Girls' Christian Mission. I Warrington has on previous occasions been favoured I with visits from the blind and crippled girls. An ' opportunity will now be afforded of witnessing the wonderful result which has attended the training imparted to these poor afflicted ones. The Parr Hall will be occupied from Monday to Saturday, October 2G-31, with an exhibition of flowers made by the girls, of whom there are 250 blind and crippled, in the London home. His Worship the Mayor (Alderman J. Smethurst, J.P.) will preside at the opening ceremony on Monday, at three o'clock, and on the following days the exhibition will be open from 11 a.m. to 9.30 p.m. daily, admission free. The girls will be seen at work making the flowers, and those who visit the hall will be astonished, as well as delighted, with all they see there. Local ladies will preside at the stalls, music will be provided at intervals, and an opportunity will be afforded of purchasing artificial flowers of all kinds at prices within the reach of everyone, and thus help- ing on an excellent work. Mr. Alfred Troughton, 28, Lovely-lane, is making the local arrangements for the exhibition, which promises to be a great success.

REV. JOHN YONGE. Seventieth BirilTdisT Celebrated at Wycliffe.

Yesterday was the seventieth birthday of the Rev. John Yonge, the respected pastor of Wycliffe Congre- gational Church, and the happy event was fittingly celebrated by the members of his congregation last evening. An At Home" was held in the Memorial Building, when Mr. and Mrs. Yonge received hearty congratulations from several hundred friends. After- wards there was a meeting in the Church, presided over by Mr. Ziba Armitage. Amongst others present were the Revs. T. Willis (secretary of the Lancashire Congregational Union), W. Bracecamp, C. Harvey Cook, J. F. Matthews, Harold Spencer (London), John Gunnell, H. Wall, J. B. Bugloss, J. S. Hughes, and J. L. Milne, Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the Warrington Free Church Council), and the officers of the Church. Mr. EDWARD WALLINGTON read a number of interest- ing letters. One was from Mr. Yonge's old congrega- tion at Heywood and conveyed the heartiest good wishes. Mr. F. Monks wrote: Mr. Yonge has given of his best not only to his own congregation but to all the public institutions of the town with which he has been connected. I think it is now more than thirty years since he came to live amongst us' and during the whole of that time he has given himself most devotedly td the work of the Master Whom he serves with such loyalty and affection. Letters were also received from Mr. E. J.Guest, Mr. W. J, Burnarn, of Evesham, and Mr. John Chater, of Liverpool, and a telegram from Mr. William Crosfleld, Liverpool. . . .

_ The CHAIRMAN said the occasion was one of great interest and was worthy of some note. Their pastor had reached the allotted span of human life, nearly half of which had been spent in the service of Wycliffe Church. If some of their friends thought there were troublous times in store let them take courage by that meeting and the feeling of unanimity concerning their Master of which it was the expression. He knew of no trouble ahead excepting one which they could all fore- see and that was connected with the future retirement of Mr. Yonge which might come at any time within the next ten or twenty years. (Laughter.) True he had been with them for thirty-two years but they had read of much longer pastorates.—(Applause.) Mr. GEORGE WALLINOTON,on behalf of the members, deacons and officers of the Church wished Mr. Yonge many happy returns of the day, and voiced their high appreciation of his work. When he came to Wycliffe they had great expectations of him—expectations which had been fully realized. The Rev. HAROLD SPENCER (London), referred to the rare qualities of Mr. Yonge's sermons andto the robust- ness and manliness of his teaching. Congratulatory speeches were also delivered by Mr. DAVID OWEN (for the Suuday School), Mr. HOLLAND (for Cockhedge Mission), the Rev, T. WILLIS (for the Lancashire Congregational Union), Mr. C. E. PARKER (for the Free Church Council), and the Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS,(for the local Nonconformist ministers.) The Rev. JOTIN YONGE feelingly replied and expressed his cordial thanks to the congregation for the loyal support accorded to him. Daring the evening a capital musical programme was submitted.

COUNCILLOR BALL'S CANDIDATURE.

Opposition's Disgraceful Tactics. Lively Meeting.

Disgraceful scenes were witnessed at a meeting held in Hardy-street on Thursday evening in support of the I candidature of Councillor James Ball in St. John's I Ward. It was evident from the outset that there was an organized opposition, and the speakers had to con- tend with a constant firs of interruptions. Women and Qhildren were largely responsible for the disturbance. They tried to drown till voices of the speakers by making as much noise as possible with rattles and trays, while fireworks, stones and till cans were directed at the platform. Nevertheless the promoters of the meeting were undaunted, and although at such a great disadvan- tage they managed to state their views and reply to Tory misrepresentations. The interruptions went on from the beginning to the end of the meeting. There was a very large audience, and the tactics resorted to by the opposition did not seem to prejudice Coun- cillor Ball's position—in fact the feeling of the great majority of the men present appeared to be one of dis- gust that a candidate should receive such disgraceful treatment. Mr. JOSEPH PODMORE, Jun., presided and spoke highly of Councillor Ball's intellectual qualifications I and moral character and repudiated the insinuations which had been made by opponents in endeavouring to identify him with Mr. Blatchford's views on religion. Councillor Ball, he pointed out, was a Christian worker and had spoken with great acceptance at places of worship in the neighbourhood. Mr. Podmora urged the necessity of backing up the Labour party's influence in Parliament by Labour representation on local bodies, so that the laws could be administered in the interests of the workers. Mr. J. HAMPSON, the Socialist candidate for Orford Ward, spoke on general labour topics and emphasised the advantages of a municipal coal supply and public control of various undertakings. Councillor B.u.r. replied to a Roman Catholic ques- tion on the subject of religious education. He pointed out that there was no religious difficulty in Warrington as children were able to be brought up in the faith of their parents in the denominational schools, while in the Council schools there was a religious syllabus which had the approval of all parties. He expressed himself in favour of administering the Act in the fairest possible way and his answer appeared to give general satisfaction. The candidate proceeded to defend his attitude in the Council on various questions concerning the interests of labour. He strongly repudiated the insinuation of atheism, pointing out that he had actually addressed the Bible class at St. Peter's Church on several occasions. He could not compli- ment the Conservative leaders on allowing such an insinuation to go forth, knowing, as they must, that it was false. Councillor Ball replied at some length to statements by Mr. W. P. Rylands. That gentleman had stated that the producer had the first charge upon all production. That was exactly the ground work of Socialism and he was glad to see that Mr. Rylands was coming over to the Socialist side. (Laughter.) Mr. Rylands had stated that he did not want the Council to be composed entirely of Con- servatives. Why, therefore, did he want three Con- servatives to represent St. John's Ward ? It was necessary to have men in the Council who could express their views • intelligently, as much of the work was done in committee and a Councillor must be able to voice his opinions there. At the close of the meeting the men in the audience were asked if they would vote for Councillor Ball and I there was a practically unanimous response of Yes." ' Councillor Ball's candidature has received the endorsement of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, representing some thousands cf workers, and he has had oilers of help from other organization.

The Rechabites and Councillor Broadhurst.

[To THE EDITOR "EXAMINER."] Sir,—Having had already a large number of inquiries from the members of the Branch of the Independent Order of Rechabites of which I have the honour to be the Secretary and also from members of other branches iof the Order n Warrington District, asking if some- thing cannot be done with respect to the attitude of Councillor James Broadhurst in giving his support during the corning municipal elections in Warrington in the favour of the candidate of a Publican as being a direct violation of the covenant and rules of our Order of which tie is a member. I have been instructed by the Executive of my Tent to request you to publish through the columns of your valuable paper, for the benefit of our members and also for our temperance friends and workers in Warrington, that Councillor James Broadhurst some months ago, by a resolution of the members of Charity Tent, ceased to be a member of our branch 'of the Independent Order of Rechabites. WILLIAM WRIGHT, (Signed on behalf of the Executive). 30, Marslahouse-lane, Warrington, October 22nd, 1908.

CITIZENS' TEMPERANCE LEAGUE.

Questions for the Candidates.

The Warrington Citizens' Temperance League has sent the following to each candidate : The Warrington Citizens' Temperance League views with concern the large representation of the Licensed Trade upon the Town Council and inasmuch as the excessive consumption ot intoxicating liquors in the County Borough is a cause of much of the poverty and degradation existing in our midst—involving a heavy burden upon the ratepayers and the community gener- ally—and as the Town Council are entrusted with the duty of controlling the police and enforcing the Licensing Laws, will you kindly give your answers to the following questions:- 1. Will you, if elected, vote against anyone con- nected with the liquor trade being placed upon the Watch Committee, whose duties are so largely concerned with the control of that trade? _ _ _

2. If elected. will you use -;o-t;r To-siii-on. for the better administration of the existing Licensing Laws? 3. As licensed properties in the Town are notoriously

under-assessed, are you in favour of their

re-assessment. Awaiting your reply on the form herewith, to cover which we enclose a stamped addressed envelope. We are, Dear Sir, Your obedient servants, The Warrington Citizens' Temperance League, G. A. LEACH, Hon. Secretary. Warrington, 23rd October, 1908.

A HORSE WARRANTY. -- Warrington Grocers Obtain Damages.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, N.C., Messrs. Edwin Richardson and on, Limited, wholesale grocers,Bridge- street, Warrington, brought an action against George Barnes, Beech Tree Inn, Barnton, near Northwich, for £ll 15s. Bd. damages for breach of warranty in respect of a horse. Mr. William Proctor, barrister, Liverpool (instructed by Mr. E. Draper, on behalf of Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.) appeared for the plaintiffs and Mr. William Madden, barrister, Liverpool (instructed by Messrs. A. and J. E. Fletcher, Northwich) repre- sented the defendant. Plaintiffs' case was that being in want of a pony in Juno of this year they heard that the defendant had one to sell. Mr. Jackson, secretary and director of the company, went over to Barnton and saw the defendant with the result that the pony was brought to Warring- ton on June 24th. In conversation with Mr. J. E. Richardson, managing director, the defendant denied that anything had happened to the animal to cause him to sell it after he had had it for three weeks and he further stated that the pony was sound in every respect. Mr. Jackson drove the pony round the cannons at Arpley, a warehouseman named John William Gregson, also being in the trap, while Mr. Richardson and the defendant watched the pony to see how it would go. Subsequently in the yard at the Roebuck Hotel defendant repeated his assurance as to the soundness of the animal, in the presence of Mr. Jackson and Gregson. It was arranged that the company should purchase the pony for £25 and on the following day defendant came to Warrington and received a cheque for that amount, along with ss. for his expenses. On June 27th the pony was turned out to grass. On July 11th Mr. Richardson sent one of his men to bring it to town and from what the man told him on arrival Mr. Richardson inspected the pony and called in Mr. Hewitt, veterinary surgeon, who reported that it was blind in the right eye and had curbs on its hocks. He also stated that it was suffering from cataract in the eye and as the cataract was com- plete and covered the whole of the lens it must have been there for some time—probably not less than six months. Mr. Richardson saw his solicitor who gave notice to the defendant to take the pony back and that failing his doing so, it would be sold. Defendant refused to take it back and it was sold at the public sale of the Warrington and District Farmers Auction Company, Ltd., on July 17th to Mr. R. C. Shaw, whole- sale butcher, for £l5 4s. 6d. less 15s. 2d. commission. Plaintiffs also claimed £1 for the keep of the pony. Evidence in support of the plaintiffs' case was given by Mr. J. E. Richardson, Mr. John Jackson, John William Gregson, the warehouseman, Mr. Samuel J. Hewitt, veterinary surgeon, and Mr. Leonard Bennett who proved the sale. For the defence it was contended that when Mr. Jackson went to Barnton to see the pony he was told that it was being sold because it was too keen for Mrs. Barnes to drive and it ran away with her, so that she refused to sit behind it any longer. At the interview in the Roebuck Hotel yard, when asked as to the sound- ness of the pony, defendant stated that he knew nothing about horses, but he had bought it as sound and sold it as he had bought it. It was also stated that at the time of the sale there was nothing wrong with the pony's eyes and it had not curbs on its hocks. For the defendant's case, evidence was given by Mr. and Mrs. Barnes, and Mr. Eastwood, veterinary surgeon, Northwich. In answer to Mr. Proctor, Mr. Eastwood stated that he examined the pony on July 27th, and on that date there was a convexity of the cornea, which rendered it impossible for him to zay whether there was a cataract, consequently he was unable to contradict Mr. Hewitt's evidence as to the condition of the eye on July 14th. Defendant called seven other witnesses, including a number of farmers who stated that they saw the pony shortly before it was sold to the 'company and failed to notice anything wrong with its eyes or hocks. His Honour decided that the weight of evidence was in favour of a warranty having been given and in the absence of expert testimony in contradiction of Mr Hewitt's statemen the must conclude that at the time of sale the pony was tuffering from a cataract in the right eye and was consequently unsound. He must give judg- ment for the plaintiff for the amount claimed with costs. Mr. Madden applied on behalf at the defendant for time in which to pay the amount, and after some dis- mission his Honour made an order for payment by instalments of £2 per month.
