PALACE AND HIPPODROME

It is a long time since even this popular place of amusement ha,s resounded with such peals of laughter as are to be heard nightly this week when Karno's comedians are on the stage. Anything connected with Karma usually means fun and frolic, and in the burlesque entitled"lfummingßirds" the Karno troupe nof comedians are see at their best. The whole thing is an extravagant " take off" of the modern music- hall, and indicates considerable skill and ingenuity on the part of its author as well as performers. A miniature music-hall is erected on the stage, com- plete even to side boxes, which are all occupied with an " audience " bent on providing half the fun. The " turns " include the topical vocalist, the Swiss Nightingale, the Rustic Glee Party, the Conjuror, the Saucy Seri°, and the Terrible Turkey. They are all capital burlesques and form a humorous entertain- ment which it would be difficult to excel. The Bros. Ashley make a return visit with their barber's shop sketch, introducing plenty of boxing comedy which keeps everybody in the merriest of moods. A clever male impersonator is Winifred Hare, known as the "immaculate boy," who presents a very smart and attractive appearance and sings three songs which have considerable tune and " g 3." Dan Whitley appears as a comedian of the eccentric type and makes a hit with the song " It's for your sake, Pm singing here to-night." Baines and Reed score as tumblers and knockabouts in their epeciality " Humpsti Bumpsti," and smart work is done by Cliff and Kean, comedy artistes. The Denaro Brothers are capital juvenile equilibrists, and Lily Hayes as skirt dancer and comedienne is particularly ' pleasing. The biegcaph and the band, as usual, are excellent.

PERFECTION SOAP WORKS BAND. PRESENTATION OF THE CUP. Au interesting ceremony took place at the Palace last evening when the Perfection Soap Works Band was formally presented with the handsome Silver Challenge Cup, valued at 50 guineas, won in the recent national competition at the Crystal Palace in which the band obtained second place. There was a large audience, and the band's magnificent rendering of the test piece " Rienzi "(Wagner) was enthusiastic- ally received. Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., was amongst those present. Mr. MossE announced that Mr. J. H. Iles, who was announced to present the cup, was unable to attend, and Mr. G D. Bradbury, the manager of the Palace, had kindly undertaken the duty. Mr. Bitapsurtv, in handing the trophy to Mr. W. Taylor(secretary of the band)said that was not the first time they had had the pleasure of listening to the band's beautiful music, and he trusted that next year they would be able to say that Warrington was the i proud possessor of the premier band n England. (Loud applause.) Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., was present, and it must be a source of pride to him that the band associated with his works was the second best band in the country. (Applause).

SACRED CONCERT.

The first of a series of sacred concerts will be given in the Hippodrome to-morrow (Sunday) evening, under the auspices of the Amalgamated Musicians' Union. The proceeds will be divided between the Benevolent Fend of the Union and local charities. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week an excellent programme will be pre- sented by the following artistes:—Nat Clifford, the nimble favourite, in new and old creations ; Jessie Burnett, comedienne ; Alunroes flack and White Comedy Team; Happy Attwood, comedian ; Three Royal Temples, operatic vocalists, including Madam Louie Gilbert ; Dan Merrett, world's champion exhibition bass drummer, late of his Majesty's army; Keith and Alerson, in their hunting absurdity "The Meet"; Effie White, kaleidoscopic and fire dancer.

BRUNNER MOND'S SCHEME FOR UNE lIPLOYED. On Wednesday night Messrs. Brunner, Mond and Company, the great chemical manufacturers, officially announced an important scheme to provide employ- ment for an increased number of men. After Monday next, at their four works—Winnington, Lostock, Middlewich, and Sandbach—skilled workers, day labourers, Sto., probably totalling over 4,000 men, will work an hour less per day, additional men being engaged to work the difference. Preference will be given to men previously employed by the firm. The labourers' week will be reduced from 491 hours to 411, and the " tradesmen's " from 43 to 43.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RAILWAY TRAFFIC DISORGANISED.

On Monday afternoon the railway service in the Runcorn district was temporarily disorganised through an engine jumping the metals on the up main line at a little distance from the station. The accident occurred at about three o'clock, and the line was not clear until 6.30.

WIDNES MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

It is stated that Councillor Edwin Wood is to be opposed in Farnworth Ward by Mr. T. A. Ballough, wuo last November was rejected for Simms Cross Ward. The cantest should prove a keen one, as both are well-known gentlemen locally. DEATH OF WELL-KNOWN SIIIP CANAL PILOT 11 The death took place on Monday morning at Runcorn of Captain Henry Hill, of lireenvay-road. The deceased, who was 47 yell-4 of age, had been ailing for some time, but it was only a fortnight ago that he was obliged to take to his bed. The cause of death was typhoid fever.—The deceased captain was a first-class pilot on the Upper Mersey and Ship Canal. He had held the position since the opening of the Ship Canal. He was acknowledged to be one of the smartest pilots in the district, and was highly popular both with captains and owners on account of his efficiency and never-varying urbanity. He was never a member of the ' Pilot-age Committee, but it was well-known that the position was open to him had he cared to accept it. Prior to his pilotage days he was a master of coasting vessels. In fact, he had spent his whole life on the water. He was a very familiar figure in Runcorn, and his common sense and genial good nature made him a host of friends. He leaves a widow and nine children. —The funeral took place at Runcorn Cemetery on Wednesday.

REWARD FOR A WIDNES HERO. At the Widnes Police Court on Monday, Superinten- dent Strickland asked the chairman (Mr. J. B. Aitken) to make a presentation to Mr. James Burgess, of 43, Chelmondeley-street, Widnes, for saving the life ot James Turner, a five-year-old boy, who accidentally fell into the river on the 30th August last. As a strong tide was running, Burgess, wittwut hesitating, seeing the danger, divested himself of his cost and plunged into the river. He succeeded in bringing the boy to the river side, where he was assisted to land. The presen- tation consisted of an illuminated address, a medal, and 30s. in money, given by the Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society. The Chairman, addressing the recipient, said it gave him great pleasure to make the presentation, and expressed a few congratulatory remarks regarding his conduct, and those remarks were endorsed by Councillor Thomas Smith, who said that had the tide, which was on the ebb taken Burgess another two yards both would have been drowned. He also referred to Burgess's career in the Army, and was proud of such a citizen. Burgess heartily thanked Mr. J. B. Aitken for his kind remarks, and said he only did his duty. STRERT GAMBLERS IN WIDNES. HEAVY PENALTIES IMPOSED.

At Widnes, before Mr. Ben Brown and Mr. Max Muspratt, on Thursday, Richard Carr, of Lngsdale-road, was summoned for street betting near the Vine Chemical IVorks on the 15th.—Police-Constable Jones said that in company with Police-constable Scarborough he saw Carr take the slips and money to make Lets. Witness came out of his hiding-place and arrested him. At the police station £5 15s. in money and twelve slips were found in his possession. In answer to the charge he pleaded guilty and said "it is the last time."—The Chairman said he was carrying on a serious business. He was getting a decent living at the works, and still he was enticing these men to go on with the betting system. It was a dangerous thing for men like the defendant to find work. He would be fined £lO and costs or two months.— Walter Allen was also charged with frequenting Pear- street on the 21st for the purpories of betting. Police. constable Wrigley stated that he saw Allen take slips and money from fifteen men and two women. They gave chase and caught him in a back entry close by. At the police station £2 2s. 3d. and twenty-five slips were found in his possession. Superintendent Strick- land said prisoner had been twice before the magistrate for the same thing.—The Chairman, in imposing a fine of £25 and costs or three months, said that the last time he was before them he promised to give it up, and the leniency meted out to him had had very little effect.

At the Tyldesley Eisteddfod, on Saturday, the Earlestown Glee Society. under the conductorship of Mr. Hughes, was placed first in the choral competition. CAPTURED BY NIGHT WATCHNIAN.

At Preston Sessions on Wednesday, Henry Billings PO, labourer, Old-road, Latchford, was charged with breaking into the OfliCe of the Vulcan Foundry, Newton-10-Willows, and wounding John Higgins, night watchman. Higgins caught him, on September 251 h, in the office, and locked him in the caretaker's lodge. After sending for the palice, prisoner, the prosecution alleged, deliberately attacked Higgins with a knife, and cut his hand so badly that he had been off work since. Prisoner said he only wanted to escape. Higgins seized the handkerchief round his neck, and he endeavoured to cut it, so as to free himself. The Bench accepted prisoner's statement, and only sentenced him to fifteen months' hard labour. THEFT OF A WATCH.

At the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednes, day, John Whalen xvas charged with stealing a silver lever watch, value 405., from the dwelling-house of 5 Derby-road, Vulcan, Newton, the property of John Moss. On Sunday, September 25th, prisoner called at the prosecutor's and obtained lodgings on the assump- tion that he had to interview one of the managers of the Vulcan Works the next morning. The watch was left hanging from a nail in the wall, and after the prisoner's departure it was missed, but was afterwards traced by the police to have been pledged at a pawn- shop in Warrington. The magistrates sent him to gaol for one month.

ST. HELENS.

SOCIALIST DEMONSTRATION AT ST. HELENS. GERMAN VIEW OF UNENIPLOYED PROBLEM. MR. SEDDON AND THE GRAYSON INCIDENT.

On Sunday afternoon a great Socialist demonstra tion was held in the Hippodrome, St. Helens, and although it is one of .the largest building in the town it was well filled, the proceedings being most enthusiastic.—Dc. Sudekum, a member of the German [ Reichstag, who was the principal speaker, said they were suffering from a great deal of unemployment in , this country, and not only in the industrial towns in i 11 the North of England but n London he saw crowded 'masses of out-of-works. Things were not very different in his own country. For many years they had not had so many unemployed in Germany as this year. Germany was protected by a tariff, and a high one, and it was a common fact that in protected Ger- many as well as in unprotected England there were many hungry men out of work. He declared emphati- cally, however, that Protection was no remedy, and it could not be said that the proportion of misery was greater in the unprotected country than in the protected country. If there were differences they were due to other causes, perhaps to the higher education of the German people, but the strong Socialist movement in Germany gave the German labourer a strong self- consciousness, a feeling of self-respect and responsi- bility, and a highly developed solidarity. They would not go on an election day and vote for their enemies. (Applause.) In England they were not likely to have an Act of Coercion for Socialists, but there were other dangers. He referred to the increased danger of war. owing to Tariff Reformers. It was quite true that up to now there had been no war openly because of tariffs. " There is no doubt," he said, " that the relations between Great Britain and Germany were drifting in dangerous directions. There is areal basis for interested men to fan the small spark of suspicion to the full blaze of strife. The professional soldier, the financier, the builder of battleships, and all those politicians who in England as well as in Germany prefer excitement about their enemy across the sea to agitation on behalf of the unemployed, have started their needless —, dangerous pante. There is a real and natur 0 rivalry on the field of commerce between th two nations. England began early to exploit the world : Germany began late, but profited by England's experience. The rivalry ought to have been confined to brains and education and the application of science to industry, but by the malign interest of militarism it has become a rivalry of battleships and military expense, and something is wanted to carry home to the convictions of both peoples the fact that organised political parties and influences are vigilantly ranging themselves in opposition to those belligerent forces, and I think it cannot be done better than by the Labour and Socialist forces of both countries pledging themselves to resist further ship-building programmes and fraternising with each other as a visible proof that the anti-war forces are much stronger than is supposed in both countries. (Cheers). If it is in opposition to the will of the majority of both peoples war is impossible. (Cheers.) Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P. for the Newton division, welcomed the utterances of their friend from Germany on the questions of tariff reform and war. They had to recognise that, so far as the Industrial democracy was concerned, their quarrel was not with the German or the Frenchman ' • their quarrel was with the people whe were on their backs at the present time, the people who were exploiting their industry.—(Cheers.) The workers, with hand and brain, were beginning to recog

I nise that under existing conditions they had to strike out a new road for themselves, and their answer was that the only road was Socialism.—(Cheers.) Referring to the attitude taken up last week by Mr. Victor Grayson on the unemployed question, he said he did not believe in running his head against a brick wall ; but he gave credit to one man who was leading the Labour forces of this country, who, when he was alone, stood up for the unemployed, and he was pre- , pared to follow the lead of Mr. Keir Hardie. As far as I the unemployed were concerned, when he said it was time to strike, the blow would be struck, not by a unit, but by the whole of the Labour party acting and work- ing together. (Applause.) Replying to a question at the close of the meeting, Mr. Seddon said the Labour party asked the Govern- ment what they were prepared to do, and Mr. Asquith made a definite promise that he would lay his proposals before the House of Commons. When they had a definite promise made by the Government it was their place to do nothing until that promise was broken or carried out, wen Monday or Tuesday came. (Hear, hear.)

TRAFALGAR DAY.

Alderman Bennett's Essay Competition.

Wednesday was Trafalgar Day and England once more paid homage to the memory of our great naval hero Nelson, and his memorable victory and death at Trafalgar. At Warrington the.occasion was marked by the presentation of the prizes annually offered by Alder- man Bennett for the best essays by pupils at the local day schools on a selected topic emphasising the out- standing lesson of the anniversary—" Duty." This year the subject chosen was" My duty to Warrington " and some very creditable essays were submitted. In the elementary schools the honour of becoming the holders for twelve months of the shield presented by Alderman Bennett is eagerly sought. So close was the competitlon on this occasion between scholars representing the Arpley-stre2t and St. James' schools that the trophy will be held by each school for six months in turn. In addition the successful essayists received book prizes. The Secondary School does not compete for the shield but the pupils there had an equally strong incentive to , excel in essay-writing, as Alderman Bennett offered some interesting books to the winners in the upper school, and the principal (Mr. E. S. A. Robson) supple- mented these with two awards for the most successful competitors in the lower school.

/tr.:Lerman BENNETT first visited the Secondary School and had an enthusiastic reception. He was welcomed on behalf of the scholars by Mr. Robson who also offered some general criticisms on the essays. Some of the writers, he said, treated the subject too gener- ally, 'while others seemed to imagine that they were on the Town Council and that it was their duty to erect new buildings and improve the town by increased electric lighting and other means. Others divided their essays into three parts—duty at school, duty when they entered upon practical life, and duty when they grew up to manhood and womanhood. In some cases that idea had been well worked out but some of the essayists were too careful to drag in almost everything as a matter of duty, with the result that their easays had become rather wearisome to read. There was a danger of their becoming humbugs because they wrote things which they would probably never think of carry- ing out. Mr. Robson caused some amusement by relating bow nearly all the 250 essayists emphasised that it was their duty to pick up paper which they found lying about and thus help the work of the Beautiful Warrington Society. In order to test them he put some paper in the playground and it remained there for several days but no one ventured to pick it up. (Laughter.) Alderman BuxNurr then delivered an address in which he emthasised the lessons of the day and pointed out that it was expected of Englishmen that they should do their duty in every walk of life. With regard to the successful essays at that school, he was very pleased with them and thought they reflected great credit on Ur. Robson, the teaching staff and the scholars. One of the most inspiring motives for doing one's duty to Warrington was its past history which they should all I read, for it would make them feel proud of the town. 11 They were indeed "citizens of no mean city." The I Alderman referred to the distinctions achieved by War- rington's sons in many branches of life and proceeded to point out that although Warringtonians had great causes for pride there was much in the town of Nvhich they should be ashamed. He alluded to the horrible slums, which were hardly fit for pigs to lire in and yet many of our brothers and sisters had to spend their days in them, the smoky skies, hideous houses, and the drunken men and women whom we sometimes saw about the streets. The town, however, with all its virtues and defects was just a reflection of its inhabitants and the more public spirit and good living were promoted the more would it improve. Alderman Bennett men- tioned that one of the essayists in the elementary schools declared he would like to be "Lord Mayor of Warrington " while another aspired to representing the town in Parliament.

The two successful essayists in the upper school wer Ethel Shepherd, 63, Manchester-road,and John Sephton I.OS, Legh-street, Earlestown. Alderman Bennett pre seated them with the following books : -.4 Walks abou Warrington" (Beamonti, " Warrington as it was, is and might be," "The Warrington Volunteers," and " The most interesting house in Warrington." The best essays in the lower school were written by Norah Vaughan, Belmont, Latchford, and Arthur E. Wright, 1, Nlarbury-street, Latchford, and they were presented by the principal with the books 'Life of Queen Alexandra ' and "Battles of the British navy "respectively. Mr. J. Moorm MURRAY (director of education) moved a vote of thanks to Alderman Bennett. He urged upon the scholars the negative side of the subject under consideration—that there were many things which they should not do. He complimented them upon their behaviour in the street and said they should always try to be a model to other children.

iThe vote was enthusiastically passed. In the afternoon Alderman Bennett visited Arpley- street schools and formally presented the shield which will be hold by the school for six months and then be handed to St.James', Latchford. The winning essayist was John Muir, 131, Parker-street, who received the following set of books:—" The Dr.= of a War- ringtonian," " Warrington as it was, is, and might be," "The Warrington Volonteers,"and"The most interesting house in Warrington." Alderman BENNETT addressed the scholars and was cordially thanked on the preposition of Mr. C. JACKSON HoL!Es (one of the managers) seconded by Mr. E. WALLINGTON (headmaster). The Alderman then proceeded to St. James', Latch- ford,and presented a similiar set of books to Lucy Tole, 6, Canterbury-street, who receised an additional prize from Mrs. \V. Bibby. .

A vote of thanks iN-Cas accorded to him on the proposi- tion of Mr. NV. H. STANSFIELD (headmaster), seconded by the Rev. IL IL CORSRIE (curate). At each school national and patriotic songs were rendered.

CROSS-COUNTRY RUNNING.

WARRINGTON ATHLETIC CLUB.

The Warrington Athletic Club held an ordinary rim from the Bridge Inn on Saturday last. The route taken was the usual club course through Arpley fields over Walton arches across the fields to a point opposite Fiddlers Ferry,then turned left to Moore through Walton and home by Borough-road. Ihe novices figured very prominent and there will evidently be some keen racing in the novice races which will shortly be held. At the annual general meeting of the Northern Cross-Country Association held at Southport, it was decided to run this season's championship at Doncaster in February next.

CASTLE V. WARRINGTON WESLEYAN lIARRIERS.—An inter club race between these clubs took place at Warrington, on Saturday. The trail was laid by J. Ashley (Castle), A. Beswick and NV. Drury (Warrington) over a rough country of eight miles, being from Siver- street schoolroom through Bewsey, across the fields to Burtonwood, coming round by way of Winwick, and finishing the last two miles on the round through Dallam Forgo' back to the club's headquarters. A very exciting finish resulted in the Castle Harriers winning by two points, which is a very good performance away from home. Warrington club was represented by 18 members and Castle by 14. PARR'S BANK EXTENSION. Parr's Bank of late has been energetic in absorbing other institutions, and the company now announces that as from June 301ast it will take over the business of the Whitehaven Joint Stock Banking Company, The latter institution was established as far back as 1829, and became a limited liability company in ISB3. Its prosperity has been well-known, as is evidenced by the fact that for the past four years the distributions have been at the rate of 24 per cent, per annum, and even more has been paid at times. In payment for the business Parr's Bank proposes to hand over 3,561 shares of their bank and £11,020 in cash,and by its acquisition it will obtain the goodwill and head office of the old bank, and banking premises at eight other places in Cumberland and Westmorland. The purchase price for the institution regresents £71,`250 in Parr's Bank shares which, at the present market price of S 5, represents a valuation of £302,940. Adding the £11,020 paid in cash it would seem that the holders of the Whitehaven shares, amounting to .C 60,150 will receive cash soc. shares equivalent to 013,960.

GREAT TEMPERANCE MEETING. Parr Hall Crowded with Supporters of the Licensing Bill.

Speeches by the Dean of Carlisle, Lin J. A. Sed-don, UP.. Principal Warman, Rev. F. A. Screeton, Rev. A. J. Palmer, and. others.

The second great meeting promoted by the War- rington Citizens' Temperance League in support of the Government Licensing Bill was held in the Parr I Hall on Monday evening, and bath in regard to attend- ance and enthusiasm. was a complete success. The hall was well filled in every part, the excellent speeches delivered were listened to with keen interest and approval, and the resolution in supprt of the Bill was carried with unanimity and great hearti- ness. His hearers would recall the report of that great Commission over which Lord Peel pre- sided and in the forefront of that report, signed by representatives of the trade, they found words to this effect : " So gigantic an evil exists in our midst that hardly any sacrifice would be too great if it could only mitigate it or make it disappear." That was their own opinion—there was a gigantic ovil,without qualification. Thera was another sentence which said that temptation *as inseparably connected with the public-house. What did they mean by that? Did they mean that the public- house was a tempting place for teetotalism, for higher life? On the contrary, what they meant was that the temptation to evil was inseparably associated with the public house. Here was a trade which admitted that it was a gigantic evil, a trade which admitted that every. single public house had connected with it inseparably moral evil. No man was more eloquent than Mr. Balfour on the evil of the trade. "You have got a trade that has existed for 33) years —a trade which is proved and admitted to be the cause of the vast majority of pauperism, crime, insality, disease and death." Mr. Gladstone said the accumulated horrors of war, pestilence and famine were not to be mentioned in the sarue breath as the horrors connected with drink. No man in his senses, coming down from the moon, would believe it possible that an enlightened State like England could stand in its midst, the exist- ence of a trade which brought about all this wreck and ruin, loss and misery. What justification was there for the existence of a trade like that in a country like ours? None. They might ask : How does it exist? The answer was plain. There was such an enormous sum of money invested in it, the trade had such enormous social and political influence and ramified so wide and deep, that it was extremely difficult to touch it. PRINCIPAL WARMAN'S ARGUSIENTS,

Principtl ‘VAILAIAN said he was a groat believer in holding temperance meetings, but he was a very much greater believer in meetings of citizens. They were proud of their canatry and the heart of the country. Their own country was worthy of fame. The presence of a Hember of Parliimmt there that night showed the signs of the times. They were not there as politicians. Some of them belongal to One party and some to another, and he did not know which he belonged tr,. (Langhter.) They were met as citizens and they were to face one of the greatest problems before the people of this country. For the last GO or 70 years they had 'hen talking, and they had 'vale thinking, and they hal been working in order that they might get one of the greatest pro'dems—tho drink problem—solved. He h n 1 seen something of the problem in the E tst 2,nd of London. Thera was one question—an ordinary level- headed question—did they want a Bill at all ? Was that Bill what they wanted ? Was the BM just ? If there was one fact they prided themselves on that was being just to a foe.

===== 0002601_19081024_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
DID THEY WANT A DILL

at all ? Ho thought it unnecessary for him to try to answer that. Ga out into their famous town of Warrington and they would find their answer n almost every straet. Oae could deal with the homes and the lives spoiled by drink. He would give a sample case. Some unfortunate men had been surrounded by the most insidious temptations— driving away the bright happy homes. Did they want a Bill ? Mr. Balfones words had been quoted and although he (the speaker) was not a politician he thought Mr. Balfour disagreed with the Bill. Mr. Balfour said in the year 1038 that among all social evils in the walks of life and in other spheres of activity the graatest of nll evils is—he did not say was, did not say bafore his Bill was passed, but after his Bill was passed—is the evil of intemperance. Sarely this was a token that they did want a Bill. Their opponents wanted to know why they could not let the drink question sleep. They hid a Bill in 1932 and 1901, but the worst of it was the 1901 Bill spoiled the 1902 one. (Laughter.) In the" city of the future "—they called themselves the " city of the future " in Birken- head—they had tried to work the 'l9Ol Act. They had got an excellent bench of magistrates; they were try- ing to make the very most of that Bill ; they had drawn a map of their town and had marked the con- gested areas and they had started to work on a section—hut not the worst by any means. His own parish had 37 licences in it,and only seven thousand people The time for them would come on about 1937. (Laugh- ter.) They had started on another area and had decided that a number of houses shoald be done away with. They were not wanted now and yet, under Mr. Balfones Act, they could not get rid of them till 1913. Showing how local magistrates were over-ridden by the Quarter Sessions, the speaker said a house in the centre of Birkenhead had four convictions against it and they decided to oppose the renewal of the license. The Bench were not teetotal fanatics. They considered the whole question and after six solid hours' patient hearing in Court they decided that it was not a fit house. The brewers with all their money in their pockets went to the Quarter Sessions at Caester. Those eminent gentle- meat living in the country villages were able to look after themselves. He did not want to say a single word against them. but ho found it hard to say anything for them. (Laughter.) The brewers were allowed by the law of the land to regain the licence. Why bad Birken- head people to 133 bossed by Chester? Was the Bill a sound one? As a matter of fact they had in the main only been talking about one section of it—they had time limit on the brain. (Laughter.) The Bill did a great deal more than arrange for the 14 years time limit. In the first place it gave them unfettered control. Warrington_would now be able to say how many public; houses it wanted, so would Birkenhead. It was a question of being allowed to mind one's own business. It meant local veto. They would have to go to the people and ask were they I I going to have one mare pnblie house? and he thought Warrington looked sensible enough judging from its I faces, for him to imagine they would all say no! (Laughter.) It gradually and effectively reduced the number of redundant houses. It was not going to rob the poor man of his beer. (Laughter.) After the 14 years was up there would be only 60,000 houses, so there would still be a chance of getting half a pint.(Laughter.) It raised the status of local Justices, it gave thorn a chance of administering the law without an appeal to the Quarter Sessions. DEATH-CHILL AT NIGIIT.

A Nurse's Extraordinary Collapse.

Chas. FOPII3S'S Bile Beans Clre Debility.

Mrs. Elizabeth Taylor, of 17, Gleba Street, Penarth, near Cardiff, is a fully-certified nurse, with many years' hospital and private practice. Five years ago, Nurse Taylor was taken suddenly ill as she was returning home from attending a patient. " I can scarcely describe the terrible feeling that cam A over me," said Hrs. Taylor to a "Cardiff Times" reporter. " I felt as if an icebarg was sanding a death-like chill right throuyit the whol.e of my body. Wy body was ice-cold, but at the same time my pulse was going at an extraordinary rate. The awful coldness made me quite blind for a time. Though I came round from this attack, I had many more of a similar nature. As a result, I became very weak; I lost all appetite, I was violently sick, and my sleep cams in fitful spells—an hour or two at a time was all I could get. I became weaker and weaker, until my friends said I was `going home' rapidly, "I had all the medical skill and attention it was posgrible to get. The doctors gave me medicine, but I got no real benefit.

POSTAL MID SAV MIS BANK REGULATIONS

For farther particulars see "Post Oflice Guide," at any Post Oilice. POSTAGE STAMPS. Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale at each post officer. Price 2s.

===== 0002601_19081024_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
INL AND CORRESPOisIDENCE. _ _ _

The term " Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, Including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence" is divided into the following classes: (/ Letters (d) Newspapers (b Postcards (e) Parcels (c Halfpenny pucketS Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size.

LETTEI POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... Id. For every additional 2 0z5.... id. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot In width, or one foot in depth. POST-CARDS. The prepaid rite of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A postcard posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 51 inches in length by 3i inckes in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 2i inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and i-inch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rnles relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter.

... HALFPENNY PACKET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 2oz. in weight. Printed matter, &0., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, Including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others isid. Newspapers not registered at the General Post (Moe are treated in the post like any other printed matter.

PARCEL POST. - The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For a parcel ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 ft exceeding 1 lb. „ 2 lb. 0 4 fl „ 2 lb. „ 8 lb. 0 5 f „ 3 lb. 5 lb. 0 6 I/ 5 lb. fr 7 lb. 0 7 ff 7 lb. 8 lb. 0 8 8 lb. 9 lb. 0 9 „ 9 lb. „ 10 lb. 0 10 „ 10 lb. „ 11 lb. 011 No parcel may exceed 11 lb. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel Is:— Greatest length... 3 ft. 6-in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring Sft. 6in. in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. 61n. round its thickest part ; a short parcel may be thicker ' • thus, if it measure no more than 3fl. in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving. PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. ' MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, 6d. ; £3O, Bd. ; £lO, 10d. By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of Advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order may be issued for places abroad varit.s in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, 6d. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 61. In addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS.

These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising hy 6d., from 6d. to Li Is. The poundage fees are up to 2s. ed.; hl. up to ]ss.; lid. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as Is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to makeyp odd sums.

- _ TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Postmaster-General possesses,almost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,oollecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United iiingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of 6d. which covers 12 words, and an additional id. for each extra word. This charge Includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but bender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or banded to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated addresg, which must consist of two words, is is.

TELEGRAMS (FonEroN AND COLONIAL) Must be wriLten on special forms endure accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK.

These are -established at all money order oilices. In ordinary MRS the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, nnri the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under bie or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is Is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps aflixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the mount of each deposit should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is El or over. a special acknowledgment is sent from the heal London °ince. Interest is allowed at the rate of 2i per cent. Any amount up to and including may be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank hook. The Examiner, Saturday October 24, 1908. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

AN OLIGOCENE SQUIRREL.

The Oligocene is one of the divisions of the ancient geological age known as the Ter- tiary. Recently the Oligocene rocks in the Bourbonnais, France, _yielded two very inte- resting little fossils, one of a bparrow, the other of a squirrel, which is almost ideLltical in all its anatomical characters with the com- mon squirrel of the present time. So per- fectly is the fossil preserved that the hairs can still be seen detaching themselves in fine lines along the back of the skeleton. The t'ead is lacking.

EXTRAORDINARY SENSE FOUND IN FISH. For more than half a century it has been understood that the lateral line of scales along each side of fishes is connected to a special nerve. The Use of these lateral organs has been a matter of much specula- tion, but the experiments of M. Hofer seem to shew that their chief function is the per- ception of water currents. They supply a new sense in place of the lacking sense of touch. They cause a 'blindfolded fish to stop and turn aside within an inch of a solid ob- ject, lead to streams and spawning brooks, akid keep the fish world informed and warned.

Sold everywhere in boxes, price I/li (56 pills) & 2/9 (168 pills). I..ll""n..M.Filani.Zl.-IrlArlarl-lrameWL.l"

===== 0002601_19081024_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
DISTRIBUTION OF ROTIFERS.

In his paper on South African Rotifers, published in the journal of the Royal Micro- scopical Society, Mr. Rouscelet remarks on the extraordinarily wide distribution of many species. • This he explains by the fact that in addition to the ordinary thin-shelled sum- mer' eggs, Rotifers also produce eggs with thick and tough shells, which can stand any amount of drying-up. These may be carried aloft with the mud of dried-up pools by the wind, and carried by air currents round the globe. During his short stay in South. Africa Mr. Rousselet was able to add largely to the list of knothi species of Rotifer in that country.

===== 0002601_19081024_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
FACTS ABOUT RAINDROPS.

A Washington official. has been collecting data as to the size of raindrops. This official employed in his experiments sheets of slate made in book form so as to be readily closed and 'ruled in inch squares. The impressions of the drops were caught on the gate and afterwards carefully copied on paper. The experiment slimed that the size of raindrops varies from a speck so ernall as to be almost invisible up to a diameter of 2in. Other in- teresting facts about raindrops brought out by this official's experiments sllttv that drops of the same size do not always; contain the same amount of water, and that some of the largest drops are hollow.

===== 0002601_19081024_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
CURIOUS LAKE PHENOMENON.

One of the natural curiosities of ',Switzer- land is Lake Morat, which turns blood red every tenth year. For centuries this freak was regarded by the Swiss with superstitious dread, and not until scientists turned their attention to the matter was the belief in the supernatural partly removed. Even now some of the more ignorant v.ersist in regard- ing the omen as one of evil import. The red- dish colour is given the water by a minute plant closely related to the red snow. Every tenth year this plant begins to propagate, i and so quickly does it grow that n a short time the whole lake is a crimson tide. The plant is so minute as not to be perceptible without the aid of a microscope, and there was some foundation for the early belief that the lake had turned into a sea of blood.

===== 0002601_19081024_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
KNEW BOTANICAL ORDER.

The two genera of seed-bearing 'plants, juliania growing in Mexico, and,. orthoplery- gium from Peru, present peculiar characters which prevent them being received into any existing order. Mr. W. -B. Hemsley, there- fore, in the Proceedings of the Royal So- ciety, has founded a new order for their re- ception under the name of julianiacese. Hitherto both the above genera have been known only from very imperfect material. They are resinous, deciduous shrubs, male and female flowers being borne on different plants. They' are small and green, or greenish-white, in colour, the former very similar to those of the oak, and the latter like those of the sweet chestnut. The fruits are samaroid, like those of the elm, and the orbicular nuts contain each a solitary seed. The new order is placed between the .nut 3 (cupuliferse) and the walnuts • (fuglandacese).

THE TWO STELLAR STREAMS.

Systematic observation of the heavens _las, h. the opinion of some astionomers, proved the existence of at least two great Stellar streams, one cf which includes our sun. Pro- fwor Dyson. has resumed . the analytical study of stellar motions, observing 1,100 stars whose proper . motions range from twenty to eighty seconds of are per century. These 1,103 stars are distributed over both celestial hemispheres. • Dyson's observations confirm those of Kapteyn and Eddington, and practically the • same apices, or objective points, have been found for the curre»te ny all three astronomers. Kobold, at the obser- vatory of Kiel, has been studying the sub- ject frorn a different point of view. He has proved that the sun is moving through space ih company with a great swarm of stars. It is the ignorance of. this fact that has intro- duced the systematic errers in the eadier determinations of the apex of this swarm.

Wigan. 1

The Wigan men brought disaster to Warrington on Saturday and their victory by 21 points to 5 at Wilderspool is about a record for the two clubs. It was expected that the home team would have a good share in the scoring, bat the visitors displayed brilliant form throughout. Warrington were certainly handi- capped by the injury to Dickenson, and the three- quarter line was disorganised. O'Neill the Welsh International forward, made his first appearance for Warrington. The match, as usual, proved a popular one and there was a crowiti of 9,C00 a couple of thousand coming from Wigan. Excellent weather conditions contributed to a fine exhibition of the handling code. Teams:— Warrington.—Tilley, back ; Fish, Cartwright, Taylor, and Landor, three-quarter backs; Brooks and Jenkins, half backs; O'Neill, Dowell, P. Boardman, A. E. Boardman, Boltoai and Naylor, forwards. Wigan—Sharrock, back ; Creevey, Todd, Jenkins, and Leytham, three-quarter backs ; dleave and Thomas, half backs; Silcock, Ramsdale, Blears, Johnston, Cheetham, and Whitaker, forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Kennedy,. Touch judges, Messrs. H. Bennett (Liverpool) and 11. Farmer (Runcorn). Warrington started off in dashing style and the War- rington defence had an anxious time, Landor upsetting Leytham near the line. Cartwright broke away with a short dribble and cleared, while a smart run on the left by Fish caused an invasion of the Wigan territory. Fine punting by Sharrock drove the homesters back and some. hot work ensued in the Warrington quarter. Taylor and Jenkins collided, but the Warrington man got the ball away to centre again. In the melee Fish was hurt and remained off the field till near the interval Thomas broke away finely from the Wigan pack and kicked past Tilley. The latter was ready to save the ball when Thomas pushed him over and scored a questionable try near the posts. Leytham missed thei goal. The reverse disheartened Warrington somewhat,l but they made a strong effort through Landor, who twice raced half the length of the field but was stopped near the line. Wigan ran down again with fine passing, and from a scrummage on the line Leytham dropped a loose ball and scored. Half-time Wigan 2 tries ; Warrington nil. Warrington had Fish back again on resuming but Wigan agained forced home their advantage, and Gleave got clearly away, and handed on to Janstone when challenged,the New Zealander making no mistake with the try, which Leytham improved. Still applying the pressure, Leytham got another goal and try through intercepting a Warrington pass, whilst a minute later Thomas ran right through with a try, Leytham again gosling. Warrington effected a rally, and after Brooks had led the way to the line, be handed on the Lsndor, who crossed and gave Fish a chance to place a goal. Result :—Wigan 3 goals, 5 tries (21 points) ; Warrington 1 goal, 1 try (5 points).

Civic League of Help.

Sir,—ln connection with this latest fashionable fad I _notice that Miss Broadbent said, " If the clergy and ministers kept to their own spiritual duties there would not be half the pauperism which existed amongst them." Who would have thought that this problem which has racked the brains of statesmen so long could be solved so easily. Are we not making a mistake in keeping women out of Parliament? Who knows whether the other half of pauperism might not be disposed of,in the same brilliant fashion. Miss Broadbent proceeded to remark that they had to be chary about taking clergy- men's recommendations. In reply to which it may be said with regard to the administration of the Poor Law, if the public had confidence that the Guardians were spending the pounds wisely they would not begrudge a few shillings spent occasionally in giving outdoor paupers the benefit of the doubt. Can Miss Broadbent give us some assurance that whilst they are spending endless time and money in red tape about an extra shilling to any outdo or pauper large sums are not spent without adequate care and investigation. With regard to the Civic Leagues of Help, are they necessary from a sound utilitarian point of view? Assuming that some of our charitable organizations are not sufficiently active or well managed and assum- ing that our Poor Law Guardians in some cases are not very humane or sympathetic, what steps can the League take ? Is it supported by any weight of public opinion. Judging from the meagre attendance and half hearted response to the first invitation, the public see nothing in it, but ostentatious charity. It is work and justice the people want.—Yours,Stc., COMMONSENSE.

===== 0002601_19081024_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
The Price of Electricity.

Sir,—My Committee would be glad if you would give the following paragraph, which is taken from the Electrical Times,' under the heading of "Liverpool," a notice in your next. edition. It is interesting to observe the comparative ages of Liverpool and War- rington Electricity Undertakings. Liverpool is in its '24th year of operation and Warrington in its 7th year. "The charges for electric energy are likely to form a subject of discussion during the forthcoming municipal ! elections. A start has been made by one of the local I papers in a leader comparing the prices charged at I Liverpool and Warrington, showing that light- ing and power consumers in the former town are not so favoured with the cheap electricity as those in the latter place. As far as lighting is concerned there is little to choose, though with the discounts allowed and the absence of meter rent, perhaps Warrington consumers are in the better case. Power lasers in Warrington, however, have a distinct advan- tage, and the paper in question urges the Liverpool , Electricity Committee to follow the Warrington Authority's lead and adopt a somewhat similar scale of charging and discounts."—Yours truly E. V. L. MATHIAS, Borough Electrical and Tramways Engineer. P.S,—My Committee thinks this paragraph is an adequate answer to certain people in this town who are alviays ,grumbling about the price of electricity and fail to appreciate that they are very well off in this respect.

Warrington Musical Society.

"Sir,—ln the hope of enlisting the sympathies and more practical interest of all lovers of music and others lii the Warrington Musical Society, I would ask you to kindly grant me space in order to direct special atten- tion to the Society's work and to solicit greater support. It may not he generally known that the Society was formed in 1834, the preliminary meeting being held in December, 1833. During this-75 years the Society has seen many changes and passed through many vicissitudes, but space will not allow of my entering at length into these. I hope, however, shortly to be able to draw up a ceficise history of the Society and with your permission, to publish it in your columns. One of the most important of these changes resulted in the appointment of Mr. F. H. Crossley, as conductor in September ISOO, and under his very able and tactful leadership, the Society has progressed numerically and musically by leaps and bounds. It now possesses a very efficient and nearly complete orchestra and stands in an almost uniq le position among such Societies. It must be always borne in mind that the oNect and scope of the Society is purely educational, and it may be fairly claimed that the work it has done has been very snccessful from a musical point of view and has also been very beneficial to the tow socially and in other ways. The recent performances of a Merrie England " will be fresh in the minds of your readers. This was pro- duced under the auspices of the Society by its opera Committee and performed solely and entirely by its members most successfully. That such a performance has been possible (not to mention previous operas pro- duced) is due to a very large extent to the long-con- tinued and successful work of the Society, and Warring- ton has a right to be proud of it and of such artistes as Messrs. J. Babbitt, J. W. Bibby, C. P. Gough,' G. H. Smith and W. Moores, and Mrs. Booth, Misses Henshall, Houghton, Anderton and many other members I am unable to name for want of space have proved theruselves,and who have given so mach of their time and have worked so whole-heartedly and with such good feeling in the interests of the Society. Seeing then that the Society's work, seconded by much natural talent and the beart7 co-operation of its ' members ho s borne such excellent fruit. I now appent' most strongly to your readers and Warringtonians generally and ask them in the interests of the town and musical education (a-blob has such a refining and of elevating infisence) to enter their names as Subscribers to the Society. The loss on last season's work amounted to £45 and the Society's power for good has been greatly lessened for want of fends. The committee have in con- sequence found themselves unable to do all they would I wish to have done. The subscriptioes for the three; concerts given each season by the Society are very moderate, viz. :—lteserved dress seats (body of hall), 10s. fid.• reserved cehtre gallery, is.; second reserved. seats (Cody of hall or reserved side galleries,) "<; 3. The first concert of the season is fixed for November 11th, the plan opens to subscribers on Saturday next, October 31st at Mr. Pearson's, Bridge. I street, who will be very pleased to take the names of new subscribers whose support would be greatly valued —I remain, sir, yours sincerely, JOHN P. REYNOLDS

Hon. Treasurer.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

INSANE COMPETITION DISCUSSED.

"How do deal with insane competition" was the problem to which our Local Parliament addressed , itself on Thursday evening, a resolution on the subject I being moved by the Rev. C. Harvey Cook. An interest- I in," debate resulted and the rev, gentleman had the satisfaction of carrying his proposals which amounted to placing more trust and confidence in one's fellows and introducing ethics into trading relations. Mr. Cook argued his points with considerable skill and was warmly cheered by all sections of the House. In the absence of the Clerk of the House (Mr. IV. Mason) his duties were undertaken by the Deputy-Clerk (Mr. Nathan Cook, jun.) The Ministry was well represented, but the Premier (Mr. John Upson) had to leave when the questions had been answered. The Opposition Loader (Mr. IV. H. Stansfleld) was in his accustomed place.

QUESTION TIME. There were only two questions of which notice had been given. One of them provoked a strong and at the same tine amusing reply from the Premier. It was addressed by the member for East Belfast (Mr. Hawthorne) and read : To ask the Prime Minister if he has authority from the Prime Minister of the Imperial Parliament for the proposals pat before this House, or are they pat before the House merely as catch-votes, and such as would not be put forward by any party in a position of less freedom and more responsibility. The PREMIETt said that a contemporary man of letters, a great Frenchman, had said in his ironical fashion that to think was a great infirmity. He con- gratulated the Member for East Belfast that he did not suffer from that infirmity. (Laughter.) He believed that on the introduction of the Budget the member for Northwich called attention to the obvious fact that he had no brains to spare—(laughter)—but that if he had any of that happy superfluity he would have conferred some of his super-abundance upon the front Government Bench. (Laughter.) "'I will suggest to him " continued Mr. Upson " that if he is ever in the position of having an abundance of that very valuable commodity, he would find those very much nearer to himself were in greater need than the members of this Bench." (Laughter.) Mr. Upson charactJrised the suggestion as to consulting the Prime Minister of the Imperial Parliament as ridicalous, when they bore in mind that the Society was simply formed to encourage public speaking and to facilitate that object the 'Government introduced Bills. Further, he was astonished that any member of the House had studied history and politics to such little effect as to think that beneficent legislation ever proved of a vote-catching character. It was not great reforms that caught votes. It was cheap ale as a rule. (Laughter). He could say fearlessly that were the Government's position one of greater responsibility and less freedom, they would still endeavour to the utmost of their powers to carry their views into effect _ _ _ _

by legislation. -(Cheers.) Mr. flAweetonNe. (E. Belfast) : The Prime Minister I knows perfectly well that although Bills on Home Rule and so on have been carried here, his party in the Imperial Parliament dare not introduce them. The Sees.= (Mr. C. Plinston) said that in asking questions it would be advisable for hon. members to use a bit of discretion. (Hear, bear.) Had the ques- tion been submitted to him after the last sitting he would have ruled it out of order. TICE MEMBER you PORTSMOUTH asked the Chan- cellor of the Exchequer : As the livings of many of the clergy are derived from mining royalties, will the Government make arrangement for the assessment of such incomes at the nine.penny rate as earned income Die CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHRWER (Mr. E. England) said he was sorry to learn that so many of the clergy derived their incomes from mining royalties, but he might say at once that as the Liberal Govern- ment was no respecter of persons, it was not their inten- tion to alter the present system, because it was working well and equitably. DEBATE ON COMPETITION. The Rev. C. Hanve.v COOK (Ashford, Kent), then introduced his resolution to facilitate humane competi- tion. He took it that it was agreed amongst the mem- bers that the present state of competition was an insane condition altogether. But there was this peculiar and striking fact : that whilst this insane competition had been growing, there had been growing alongside of it a large public spirit which had tried to battle, and somewhat successfully he hoped, with this insane com- petition and to make it more humane. To advocate the abolition of the principle of competition was to run counter to and to run one's head against the stone wall of experience and of history. The competitive principle, so far as his reading of history was concerned, was indestructible in human nature. Neither did he favour State regulation of competition, because it did not appear to be anything more than a make-shift. His sug- gestion might sound-a little quixotic, but it was that in order to solve the problem they should appeal to the msgnarnimity of rnen,that they should place faith in men and have confidence in each other, that they should put ethics into trade and make the principle of faith in men the regulating principle in all they did in com- merce. Man in the hulk had ever been worthy of trust and had ever responded to it. There should be no legal disabilities, liabilities or penalties in order to deal with the question. The root evil was that men did not go into business for a life's work, but simply to make money and to gain their pile as quickly as they could. He agreed that the laws or demand and supply regulated competition, but not the baneful part of it. Even these 'awe, however, were simply artificial and could be banded to new conditions. The sugges- tions he offered by which the end he had in view should be reached were:— (1) The establishment of chairs in Universities for .the study of morals as applied to business. (2) A course in business colleges on the ethical demands of trade. (3) Chambers of Commerce propaganda along the same lines. (4) The development of commercial relation- ships scientifically. In conolusion, the rev, gentleman said they wanted business men to realize that they were as much called to the ministry of trade as he was to the ministry of preaching. He formally moved a resolution embodying the above proposals. Mr. W. H. SlintaßlTllN '(Secretary for Scotland) seconded. Mr. W. NuaisTrVGALE (Normanton) speaking in his usual vigorous and earnest way, contended that if business were a more matter of money making, the metallic spirit cursed a man and the best would not come nui; of him. Collectivism, the common good, the altruistic principle, would revolutionise the world. He was a believer in the Gospel and in true preaching, but when one considered the vast amount of oppression' misery and avarice, selfishness and devilishaess which was rampant to-day, one wondered whether the preach- ing of the trowel had pot so far been a failure. They could not trust human nature. They could not trust each other, or why have any l tws ? Society was bound to protect itself for -the common good. The curse of England was not the lack of tariff reform, but the lack of common sense in the management of ifs business. Socialists declared : " Give us a chance, and if there is any good in human nature we will get it out of them." They could not get good under the present competitive system. It was wicked and evil. Mr. J. Ileums (President of the Loon Government Board) contended that a modification and re-organiza- tion of our industrial system would be necessary before ' the priuciples enuueiated by the Rev. Harvey Cook could be pet into practice. Mr. W. KILDUFF (Dublin Harbour) said Christianity I was the only anti-dote and no solution would be of any value unless it wore animated by the Christian spirit. He was struck with the great intentions of the Socialists, but he asked them to abolish one plank in their platform—secular education—because it was increasingly necessary that the truths of Christianity should be inculcated in the rising generation. Mr. S. WFUGHT (Derby) declared that he had to a very great extent lost all confidence in man. He characterised the Limited Liability Act as the greatest curse which ever came into the country, for it had enabled capitalists to combine and crush out the small man. He did not consider the Rev. Harvey Cook's

proposal.; were practicable. Mr. W. CHRIME3 (S. Birmingham) agroal that the introduction of limited liability companies had had a great deal to do with increasing severe competition. The Idea that there should be no legal penalties was absurd, because we had to deal with human nature as it is, and not as it might be or should be. It was a fallacy to think that cheapness was generally a benefit and that idea lay at the root of unreasonable competi- tion. Mr. Chrimes' suggestion that the proposals of tariff reform were directed to scientific trading was greeted with great laughter, and caused the hon. member to protest against "the school- girl tittering and giggling " with which references to tariff reform were received. A large number of mem- bers of the House appeared to know as much about tariff reform as they did about the transit of Venus. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Carimes pointed to Germ my as a nation which adjusted its trading relations scientifi- cally. (A Nationalist : They have tried about fifty times and have not got it right yet). That was entirely beside the mark. (Laughter.) Mr. Chrimes gave the Rev. Harvey Cook credit for all sincerity and a desire to improve matters, but he thought the House had a right to expect practical proposals, which they had not got in this case. Mr. W. H. SIIRREURN (secretary for Scotland) said the Rev. Harvey Cook's conclusions were such as could only have been come to by any Christian minister who was true to his Christianity. He went all the way with the rev, gentleman in the ballet that in industry they should do everything from the highest motives and for the love of the work. They must come to first principles and apply them to the industrial world. Labour, he maintained, should have its full share of the profits. Mr. W. J. WALKER (Stafford) said no true capitalist believed in under-paying his men, because he knew that under-paid labour was the worst he could have. The honest capitalist, the man who put his money Into a business and worked, was entitled to that which lais business made. The competitive principle was indispensable in human nature and he maintained that the canker which was eating at the heart of our industrial system was not competition or the fact that

REV. MALDWYN HUGHES AT BOLD-STREET.

Address on " Progress."

In connection with the Wesleyan Methodist Bible Class League a public meeting was held last Thursday evening, in Bold-street Church, when a good number present listened to an eloquent address on" Progress " by the Rev. H. Maldwyn Hughes, 8.A., 8.D., of Liver- pool.—The Chairman (Mr. C. B. Parker) was supported by Mr. J. C. Holland (president of the League), Rev. Thomas Miller, Mr. J. Upson and Mr. J. Maile.—Mrs. A. Booth rendered "I know that my Redeemer liveth " to the satisfaction of all.. _ _

Mr. C. E. PARKER said heves pleased to be a leader of a class that formed the Eeagne. The League had great possibilities—not only possibilities of doing useful work to those who were associated with it, but to other people in the town. Ho was interested in the subject Mr. Hughes was going to speak about—progress. In thinking of it one was impressed by the wonderful progress made during modern time. The progress they had made was not of an equal character. Perhaps the most striking development in material things were inventions, manufactures, and in harnessing forces for work in various ways. Those things that had made the progress should have lightened the toil of man, but had not done so, as they might have expected. They might have reasonably expected, that if it had not enabled them all to be lifted up to a state of luxury, it would—at any rate—have lifted everyone of them out of a state of want. It had undoubtedly made the burden of getting the daily bread easier, but to the bulk of the poorer classes it had left them poorer in , power, and poorer physically and mentally, and their I advantage to progress was little better, if any better, than it was before the development began. It was a problem which had to be solved and which concerned the race and the heart of all those who loved their country and kind. (Applause.) The Rev. H. MALDWYN HUGHES' in rising to give his address on " Progress " said he was specially. glad to be associated for an evening with his old friend Mr. Miller. They were at college together and spent a very happy time. He rejoiced to know of the good work that he was doing among them. Were their Bible classes doing any good ? Was the church doing any good ? Was anything doing any good? Was tke world getting any better ? he asked, or were they ploughing the sands ? after All the efforts that had been put forth by the Church of Jesus Christ in all its branches, after the toil and sacrifice and the pouring out of money what was the result ? Was the world any better after all their efforts? There were some who were inclined to think that the hope of progress was a delusion. In the middle of last century there was a great hope. Then hopes were being born. It was a time when great advance was made in science—greater in the last century than in all the centuries gone before. Cries of hope were in the air and there was a great popular movement. Large extensions of the franchise were made and in every direction there was activity. There was legislative activity, and laws of a beneficent character were written on they Statute Book and man began to think that the golden age was about to dawn, To-day men were disappointed. There was no disguising the fact that their hopes had not been realised. The discoveries of science and the invention of labour saving machinery had proved in reality to be man saving machinery. Last century saw the great depopulation ot the villages, ' when people rushed to the towns, with the result of an increase in slums &c. They learned the secret of pro- ducing wealth, but they never learned the secret of distributing it. (Hear, hear.) The country was never richer than it was to-day and yet there appeared to be more unemployed. They ought to rejoice that the Government were doing something to deal with the question. (Hear hear.) " The " Daily News " suggested that morning that if there was to be any improvement they must abolish child labour and the half-time system. It was this sort of thing that was weakening the national character. Legislation had not done all that it was hoped it would do. The Houses of Parliament had not had the time to pass all the laws for which people were claret:miring. There never was a time when the struggle for life was so keen and the extremes of poverty and wealth so manifest. Mr. Bryce's conclusions amounted practically to this "That the hope of progress is a delusion and that the golden age was in the past. First of all there came a period of advancement then one of retrogression and then came another and so on for endless ages ' • and man could do little to force his way, and the mist which surrounded him at the beginning of the voyage was there still." All Mr. Hughes could say to that was, if it were true then Christianity was the biggest failure the world had ever seen. If ever Christianity did one thing more than another, it was to assure them that the best days were in front of them and not behind them, and the golden agehad yet to come. What was the truth of -matter ? Was Mr. Bryce or Christianity right? If they were to answer that they must have some test of progress. Of course, men might try different tests. They might apply the test of advance in science, of wealth, and of industries. They might claim as a sign of progress of a nation that it possesses the largest steamships that cross the Atlantic. Ha believed that advance in every direction was practically through the operation of the Spirit of God. The test Christianity must apply was this: Is the individual, the nation and the race conforming itself to the will of God ? That was the test they must apply as well as history. They would fled that it. was a test of progress. The decline of other nations was because other people, stronger than they, arose and beat them. They were overthrown from within as wall as by the enemy. Were they as a nation. or as a race, becoming conformable to the will of God, or were they antagonistic to the Divine will ? He believed there had not been a time in the history of I the world when the Spirit of God was so manifest as at I present in human affairs. It was the same in national and municipal affairs. He did not think that in the whole course of the history of Parliament—not even excepting the time of Cromwell's ascendancy— there had ever been a House of Commons so anxious to embody in legislation the will of God as the present House of Commons. (Hear, hear.) He was one of those who could see the guidance of God in the great labour movement. He was referring to that greai; movement of the majority of the masses. It was a time to rejoice when they saw people moving away from sport and drink to justice and social emanci- pation. (Applause.) That great movement needed Christianity. H 9 knew there were some who said it did not, but there ware also some who kne v they could not get on without it. How could they stead by while that great movement of the people was taking place. (Applause.) He believed that great movement was linked to Christianity. Referring to the fault of the Church, the rev. gentleman pointed out that it was not through motive or intention, but through ignorance ; the men in the church peered out money into the central missions and for othergood objects. Ire believed in progress because he believed in the individual heart. If every symptom and sign were against him he should still believe it, and that Jesus Christ had the power to shake off the sin that bound the world.So long as they were ready to die for the Kingdom of Heaven, the Church would be immortal in the life of the nation. (Applause.) Oa the proposition of Mr. Hord, IND, seconded by Mr. Ueses, votes of thanks were accorded to Mr. Hughes, the Chairman, Mr. F. Hick uan (organist), and Mrs. Booth.

The harvest thanksgiving services were commenced at this mission on Thursday evening when a large congregation assembled. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

" By Ovalist."

When you are well beaten it is just as well to admit the fact and save further trouble. Warrington were certainly well beaten on Saturday by Wigan, and the only consolation is that the blow was not unexpected. Just now, Wigan are at the height of their fame and prowess, while Warrington, who have a much more honourable record of deeds accomplished, are passing through dark days. The process of team-building takes time, but with a substantial basis to go upon it should not be long before Warrington can hold up their heads again in the best company.

Wigan have undoubtedly got a remarkable side together after some years of lavish expenditure. The display they gave on Saturday was one of the finest seen on the Wilderspool ground for years, and the ease with which the locals were outmanoeuvred recalled the visit of Hull Kingston Rovers, when the latter were a power in the land under the leadership of Sammy Morfitt and Anthony Starks. It is possible that the Wiganers played a hit above their form— certainly one man did—but at any rate they have got one of the best teams in the country and they aro a long way ahead of Warrington. It is not long since Warrington had things pretty well all their own way with Wigan and they will probably "come again." Every dog has his day, you know. Just now Wigan are top dog.

The influence of the New Zealanders, rodd and Johnston, was plainly discernable in the play of the Wigan men. They have adopted "All Black" tactics very largely and brought the art of combination to the pitch of perfection. Todd himself has a lot to do with the effectiveness of the third line, while Johnston makes a magnificent leader (.1 the front rank. There is no doubt that Wigm are playing the KU.. game as it should be played, and all that remains for Warring- ton is to secure men with the necessary speed and resource and follow suit. On Saturday they "revoked" every time passing was attempted.

It is not long since Warrington would not have taken second place to any team in the brillihnce of their combined back-play, but the side is at present disorganised. On Saturday, the absence of Dickenson and the want of fitness of Taylor had much to do with the weakness of the third line both on attack and defence. The mings were really better than the centres, though they had little enough opportunity for Alining. It is at three-quarter where Warrington are weakest. None of the men are fast enough—none of them are as fast as they used to be—and until new blood is introduced here there will not be much improvement in general play and match-winning effectiveness. The position is critical, and the required backs must be obtained if the Club has to pledge its credit for the purpose. Enterprise is the only true economy at present, for with a poor team revenues are sure to fall. The acquisition of players who would put, the Club into a winning vein is a policy which would ultimately pay for itself. Warrington cannot afford to occupy a second-rate position. That's about the size and shape of the situation.

There was one surprising feature of Saturday's game, and that was the way the forwards were out- played. I think the club has got a good pack at last, and probably more was due to Wigan's peculiar method of packing than anything else. They were not stronger than the Warrington men but they have taken advantage of the rules to pack five down in many cases and heel out as soon as the ball is in. If another team played the same game there would be such a mix-up forward that the referee would be bound to interfere. At present he lets things go on as they are and the other side loses possession nine times out of ten. Wigan have played this scrummage trick for a couple of years now, and it is high time a determined effort was made to circumvent it. The orthodox scrummage formation will not do against the Wiganers, and I hope a little thought will be given to the subject bef ore Warrington and Wigan meet again.

It struck ma that Referea Kennedy was hardly sharp enough for the Wigan men. They did a heap of forward passing at top-speed which went undetected by the man in charge, and when the first try was scored Tilley was so plainly fouled by the scorer that the crowd could hardly credit tha fact when the point was allowed. The homa full-back was pushed off the ball when about to pick-up and the offence was obvious to all—but one !

Much interest was taken in the appearance of W. O'Neill, the Welsh International whom Warrington secured from Cardiff last week. He was, of course, strange to the rules but he gave evidence more than once of that resource and coolness which had won for him the title of the cleverest forward in Wales. O'Neill does not seem to have a robust physique, but he played the game and will, I hope, distinguish him- self ere long. Dowell, his fellow-international played a rare scrummage game, while young Boardman was always prominent. The forwards should more than hold their own in future engagements.

I was not so much impressed with the play of Brooks at the base of the scrummage as I have been when he has taken the outside-half position' and the conviction is slowly being borne upon me that he is not well-suited as a scrum-worker. With another good half to feed Jenkins, we could do with Brooks at three-quarter. Perhaps the two might change places with advantage. A point in which both were strangely lacking was in defence, and how they came to let Johnny Thomas through go often was a mystery. He played the deuce with Warrington's back division and individually gave one of the finest displays I have seen for many a long day. But he should have been checked oftener. What would George Thomas have done, think you, last Saturday?

A word of praise is duo to Landor, who did splendidly on the right wing, scored Warrington's only try, and made the two finest runs of tha day. If this young player only had more speed ho would do Warrington some excellent service. He should go in strongly for sprint-practice if he wishes to make a name for himself in N'.U. football. Tilley also came out of a trying ordeal with great credit. For a veteran he has few supariors as custodian.

Warrington's record is rather melancholy reading —two wins out of eigh t matches--and it does not seem likely to improve much before the end of November as a glance at the fixtures with show.
