TILE AUSTRALIANS AT RUNCORN.

I saw the Australians for the first time at Runcorn on Wednesday, when they beat the locals by 9 points to 7. The result was a bit of a shock to Runcorn people who imagined that the Linnets would be the first side to beat the Kangaroos. They beat the All Blacks by 9 points to nil, the only match in which the New Zealanders failed to score throughout the tour. The present team was a bit too good for Runcorn though the latter came very near to winning in the closing stages of a toughly-contested game. The Kangaroos owed a great deal to Messenger, who has played in every match so far, and kicked his usual marvellous goal from half-way on Wednesday. I doubt whether we have ever seen his equal at place- kicking or punting.

The Kangaroos did not strike me as being as clever a side as the Yew Zealanders. There is not the same brilliant combined back play, but they will be quite as hard a side to beat though they will not score so frequently. They have a better defence, are quite as speedy, and play a more robust and dashing game. liuncorn combined far better than the Australians, but the men were outpaced and seldom made much headway in mid-field. The visitors showed that they could pass well at times, and with practice they may develop into a brilliant side. There is ample individual cleverness.

The best men on Wednesday, after Messenger,xvere Bolesyski (already nicknamed " Bottle of Whisky ") a remarkably safe and speedy full back ; Dean at half- back and Devereaux at centre. The latter is a fine srverver and should get plenty of tries. Bailey made a first appearance on the right wing but had not many decent opportunities. He was very keen to make headway and being a player of Hogg s build he took a lot of stopping. In a fine fast lot of forwards none did better than the curly-haired giant, Davies, and Tom McCabe, onceof Widnes. The latter has grown immensely and is a strapping fellow now. -- Hockey. Latchford first eleven met Weaste last Saturday at Weaste and the forwards came out of their shells by scoring six times and as the home team could only net once they were heavily defeated. The goals were scored by Spensley (3) Banks, Scott, Jones, one each. We hope this kind of thing will continue.

The Second Team beat Ashley at home 6-2 the scorers being Minards (3) Hackney, Parker, Booth, one each. The visitors goals were both very good efforts and the team is much improved,

To-day the First Eleven go to Bury with the follow- ing team :—J. Appleton, goal ; C. Plinston, T. Dixon, baJks ; D. Travis, J. B. Wilkinson, J. Albinson, halves ; F. Newell, It. Jones, H. Scott, E, Banks, W. Spellsley, forwards ; referee, Mr. W. B. Cobb.

The Second Elevens meet at Latchford, and the home team is :—W. H. Pickles, goal ; J. Wilkinson S. R. na Runey, backs ; A.Newton, A. Knight, F. 'Cofe,balves; H. Booth, C. Hackney, H. Parker, W. 11. Minards, A. Starkey, forwards ; referee, Mr. Harry Warburton. Bully-off 3 p.m.

THE MAYORALTY. Town Council—Siicts Alderman

Forshaw.

At a special meeting of the Town Council held at the Town Hall on Monday afternoon, Alderman Forshaw was asked to allow himself to be nominated for the Mayoralty of the Borough and consented. The debate was somewhat lively, criticism being forthcoming of the action of the Council in placing a number of gentlemen in a competitive position for the aloe. The deputation appointed to wait upon seven members of the Council presented their report from which it appeared that Alderman Pierpoint, Bennett and Barton had declined the position and Alderman Forshaw, Councillors Dr. Joseph and W. B. Roberts left them- selves in the hands of the Council. A letter was read from Councillor Fulton-Smith, another of the gentle- men approached, protesting against the methods adopted in making a selection. A resolution proposed by Alderman TINNION and seconded by Councillor PEMBERTON that the senior member be selected was carried with about four dissentients,after which several me nbers left the room. On the motion of Alderman TINNION, seconded by Councillor WILICINSON, it was unanimously decided that Alderman Forshaw be asked to allow himself to be nominated for the position of Mayor. The ALDERMAN consented and said that if elected be would do his utmost to uphold the dignity of the office and to discharge his duties impartially. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the retiring Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) for his admirable services during the past twelve months, on the proposition of Alderman BENNETT, seconded by Alderman TINNION and supported by many rnembera of the Council. All spoke most highly of Alderman Smethurst's devoted labours, Alderman Tinnion remarking that his conduct in the chair had exceeded the anticipations of the most sanguine members.

The MAvon briefly acknowledged the oompliment. Alderman FORSHAIV, the mayor-elect, is a native of Warrington and received his early education at the National Schools, Church-street, and afterwards at the Blue Coat School. After a successful career at the latter institution he entered the office of Alderman Joseph Davies and received an excellent commercial training. He was on one occasion auditing the books of Messrs. Brook and Davies, a firm of solicitors at that time carrying on business in Warrington, when Mr. W H. Brook (a former town clerk) offered him an appoint- ment which he did not accept until the expiration of four months, but in 1378 he was appointed accountant to that firm, and ultimately in 1890 became a solicitor— the first from the Blue Coat School. Alderman Forshaw is now the head of the firm of Forshaw and Forshaw, solicitors, Suez-street. A staunch churchman, ha was for many years a member of the Parish Church choir, and became tho president of the Choristers' Association a few years ago. Ho has also held the office as church warden at Holy Trinity Church. He is the president of the Blue Coat Society, having succeeded the late Alderman Davies, and a member of the Board of i\lanagernent and a Life Governor of the Warrington Infirmary. He was elected an Alderman in the place of the late Alderman John Roberts in July 1906, the voting being, for Councillor Ellison 9, for Councillor Forshaw 13. Alderman Forshaw is in his 53rd year. In 1831 he married Miss Bessie Barlow and his family consists of one son and two daughters.

THE LICENSING QUESTION. Tied Tenants' Interests.

Prima Minister's Important Promise. On Thursday evening a deputation of members of the iberal and Labour parties waited upon Mr. Asquith in private in his room at the House of Commons for the purpose of laying before him certain amendments in the Licensing Bill, in the interests of tied tenants. The deputation consisted of the following members : Messrs. Clement Edwards, C. H. Corbett, C. P. Allen, J. Ward, J. Rowlands, E. FL Lamb, G. Radford, C. W. Bowerman. II 11. Raphael, Richardson, J. A. Saddon, A. Mond, Sir M. Levy, and Sir Charles M‘Laren. The deputation was introduced by Mr. Clement Edwards, and other speakers were Messrs. Mood Seddon, and Ward. The two points on which they laid special emphasis were : (I) That in clause 10 an amendment should be inserted making it clear that even where a licence- holder was only a manager, and not a tenant, he should be entitled to compensation. (2) That under no circumstances should licence- holders be awarded less than one year's profits by way of compensation. The Primo Mimister said that he was in sympathy with the objects sought by the deputation, and said that the Government would insert amendments in the bill providing both for the compensation of the managers and the award of compensation to the extent of at least one year's net profits to the licence-holders. The deputation expressed the greatest satisfaction with the Premier's statements.

The directors of Lipton, Limitei, have declared an inter rim dividend for the past half year on the ordinary shares of the company at the rate of sit per cent, per annum. The directors are pleased to report that both the profit and trading show a satisfactory increase in the volume of business exceeding by half a million sterling that for the corresponding six months of 1907. The ordinary share transfers will be closed from the 26th October to the oth Noveraber, 1008 (both days inclusive).

DEATH OF RR JOSEPH POTTER.

One of Warrington's best known townsmen passed away on Monday morning in the person of Mr. Joseph Potter, 5, Suez-street, who for many years had been one of the proprietors of the Royal Court Theatre, Rylands-street, and the Royal Theatre of Varieties, Scotland-road. Mr. Potter, who was 04 years of age, had been in indifferent health for several years, and the fire which occurred at the Royal Court Theatre on Christmas morning, 1006, was a great shock to him and probably hastened his end. For the past 'ten weeks be had been confined to his bed suffering from an internal complaint, his medical attendants being Dr. Fox and Dr. Fox, jun. Mr. Potter was a native of Warrington, and in his early days be was a printer. He also devoted con- siderable time to dramatic criticism, the stage always having a great interest for him, and represented the, "Era " in Warrington for about thirty years. Hisi position brought him into touch with such old-time favourites as Barry Sullivan, George Melville, T. C. King, Walter Montgomery, Charles Matthews, Madam Celeste, and other artistes. He enjoyed intimate friendship with the late Mr. Brinsley Sheridan, a leading comedian and the builder of the Theatre Royal, and it was through Mr. Potter's influence that that gentleman was induced to take up his residence in Warringtor. Mr. Potter's brother, the late Mr. Tom Potter, was at one time a prominent and successsul actor. _

The provision of various form of entertainment for the public early occupied Mr. Potter's attention. He formed Warrington's first minstrel troupe, and was himself an active member of it. Later he was associated with the late Mr. W. B. Harmston, circus proprietor, in the erection of a circus in Sankey-street on the site of the Park Bakery, and the venture met with considerable public patronage. He next engaged in theatrical enterprises with Mr. William Johnson and eventually they entered into partnership. At first they rented the old Public Hall, fitted it up for the purpose of stage plays, and engaged travelling companies. The success attending their efforts led them to go a step further and the building was purchased from the Public Hall Company. Then they amalgamated with the well-known Milton Rays, and this led to the open- ing of the Royal Court Theatre on July 28th, 1892, with a performance of " Kindred Souls,'" a great deal of money having been spent in alterations and decora- tions. The firm during the following year purchased the Theatre Royal. At the end of 1896 Mr. Johnson, having received an important appointment in Cheshire, retired from the active management of the theatres,- but resumed his connection with them last year in con sequence of Mr. Potter's declining health. The re-building of the Court Theatre, after the disastrous fire of nearly two years ago, is fresh in the minds of our readers, and it it need only be said that the theatre will now compare very favourably with any building of its size in the provinces. Quite recently, owing to Mr. Johnson's desire to retire from the theatrical business and to Mr. Potter's weak state of health, the partnership was dissolved. It was decided to put up both buildings for sale by auction. The sale took place on September 15th at the Albion Hotel, Piccadilly, Manchester. The Rylands-street house being purchased by Mr. Alderman Beecham, one of the directors of the St. Helens Theatre, and the theatre of varieties by Mr. Henry Carver, of Eccles. Mr. Potter's experience in operatic management went back to the days of Pyne and Harrison Manley and Draham, in minstrel business to Ramsey and Newcombe, I and in the circus line to Pablo, Fanque, Howes, Cushings and others. When he completed his 25th year of theatrical management in the town he was the recipient of a public testimonial which took the form of a beautifully illuminated address, tastefully framed. It expressed the appreciation of the townspeople at his efforts to promote the welfare of the community by providing an entertainment of an elevating and healthy moral tone, and added : We also desire to express our admiration of you as a man, and one whose conduct, both public and private, has been beyond reproach, and has earned approbation by all classes, and we earnestly hope that you may be long spared to enjoy the well-earned reward of your many Tears work in Warrington." The signatories on behalf of the committee were Mr. George Wright (chairman). Mr. John Lee (vice-chairman), and Mr. Fred, J. Reece (hon. secretary and treasurer.) THE FUNERAL. _ . _

_ The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday, the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, vicar of Holy Trinity officiating. Amongst those present were : Mr. Saunders (cousin), Mr. Alec Saunders (cousin), Messrs. Harry H. Potter, Herbert Potter, Leopold Potter, Walter Bellian, Percy Woods (nephews), Mr. Chumbley (uncle), Mr. Stirrup (brother-in-law), Mr. Dudley Hart (Solicitor to the executors), Mr. William Johnson (theatre partner), Mr. F. J. Reece (acting. manager, Royal Court Theatre), Mr. J. Waters (acting manager Theatre Royal), Messrs. Alfred Jolley, James Lawton' George Archer, S. Newall, John Lee, Percy Coates, H. M. Burgess, J. Reece (Royal Court Theatre), staff and band of the Royal Court Theatre, " Guardian" Office, The Pardoes and Miss Lottiel Holland, Mr. and Mrs. F. W. Willmott (Liverpool), Weisker Bros. CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY LECTURES

The Education Committee, of the Warrington Co-operative Society does a useful work in arranging interesting and instructive entertainment for the mem- bers during the winter months. The present session was successfully inaugurated on Thursday evening when Mr. 11. Hibbert, F.R.G.S., and member of he London Polytechnic Society, delivered a cinematographic lecture in the Parr Hall before a crowded audience. The subject was entitled " Picturesque Pyrenees,and Spain" and the lecture proved highly interesting. The chair was taken by Mr. T. Wadsworth (president of the Society.) Mr. Hibbert has obtained some excellent pictures during his travels in France and Spain and they con- siderably enhanced the value of the lecture. He men- tioned that it would take people three days to get to the Pyrnenees. A very interesting place was the town of Pau situated at the foot of the Prynees. Many beautiful pictures were shown of this town, including the quaint old houses, irregular in shape and yet very picturesque in appearance. Many striking moonlight effects were obtained in some of the views. The next town visited was Biarritz situated on the coast, and on the frontier line of France and Spain. Animated views of the sea water dashing up the rocks were exhibited. Leaving the sea coast the lecturer again went inland and the animated views of the mountain passes, ravines waterfalls, where nature was seen in its wildest aspect, were admirable. Many scenes in the ancient old town of Lourdes were depicted. The cathedral of Lourdes called " The Roses," presented one of the most beautiful sights imaginable. Thousands of pilgrims go .to this place yearly taking with them those stricken with disease, in order to drink the sacred waters. A series of pictures depicted the infirm people being carried to the baths, and the grounds of the Cathedral in which was a huge pile of crutches and other appliances left by the people, who had been brought to this sacred place, to be bathed and also to drink the waters, and after a few weeks' time had been able to walk to the railway station quite healed. Many miraculous cures have been wrought at the town, which doctors and physicians are unable to account for. The town of Tarbes was next visited and the audience had the opportunity of seeing a representation of a bull-fight, entirely different to the Spanish mode of bull-fighting. No weapons of any kind were used, and the bull's horns are encased in steel knobs, so as to prevent the animal from goring the toreadors. The crossing of the Pyrenees was well pictured, there being views of the customs houses where passengers' baggage is searched, to prevent any spirits, powder, etc., going into the country duty free, and after crossing these mountains the traveller found himself in the country of Spain. The lecturer observed that there was a very poor train service in Spain—in fact it is very diflifiult for a third class passenger to travel at all. The express trains travelled at the rate of about seven miles per hour, often stopping en route to pick up passengers. Many interesting views of the old Capital of Spain, , Madrid, were displayed. The old Mosques containing specimens of the old Moorish fine art were a pleasure to look upon. A Spanish bull-fight was depicted by cinematograph and the lecturer commented upon the barbarous and cruel nature of the sport. From 18 to 20 horses had been known to be slaughtered in one bull-fight. The buildings in which the fights took place were very large, seating 16,000 to 13,000 people, and many ladies attended. It was stated that the Queen of Spain would issue a decree, if the Govern- ment would allow her, prohibiting all ladies from witnessing, bull-fights. Seville, the home of the Seville orange, was next shown, and the speaker remarked that the oranges and lemons grew on the hedges there as abundantly as the wild fruit in our country. The city was also famous for its Moorish Mosques, one of which was pictured on the screen and showed the extensive work and fine art exec-11'6d by the Moors hundreds of years ago. As the lecturer remarked, the work was not done for 2s. d. but for the love of the thing. They put their whole heart into it. Views were also shown of the Royal Palace in Madrid, with the extensive court yards and grounds. The lecture concluded with a series of cinematograph views of the Franco British Exhibition. Some huumorous pictures were also shown and created great merriment. Two songs " April Morn " and" Killarney "were taste- fully rendered by Miss Marie Houghton and formed a highly popular feature of the :entertainment.

ST. JOHN AMBULANCE BRIGADE.

Anyone with only a slight acquaintance with the - work of Israel Zangwill, author of " Children of the Ghetto" etc., would expect i something out of the ordinary in a play from his pen. They will not be - disappointed, then, with "Merely Miry Ann," which is better known as a comedy than it was as a novel. " Merely Mary An " is a decidedly uncommon stage production, —uncommon in scene, in character, and in words. Yet, after all, it is simple enough in its , essentials. Mary Ann is a lodging-house slavey of a- particularly grimy and unsophisticated type. Only 3 musical genius could have been expected to see more in her than was revealed by her unprepossessing exterior. The genius is hard-up and full of revilings for a banal and unsympathetic world that prefers the treacly and sentimental in music to the inspira- tions of genius. Mary Ann, at least, appreciates him at his highest valuation, and he comes to , appreciate Mary Ann. Beneath her dirty print he discerns a heart of gold, and a nature as unspoiled as that of the homely, little-considered sparrow. Fate has, of course, other plans for these - two than fretting out their lives in tawdry surround- ings. The genius has just decided that he must bow to conviction, write music which will be treacly but saleable, and take Mary Ann with him to the fields and pastures from which she sprang. At this point Mary Ann inherits half-a-million from her wicked brother Tom, who had gone years before to America —synonymous with Hades to Mary Ann. She does not want the money and offers it to the genius if he will only permit her to serve him to the end. But the genius has a conscience, and does the right thing by Mary Ann. She is handed over to the care of a clergy- man in her old parish, educated, and launched into Society by the professional chaperone. For six years the ways of this odd pair lie apart. The genious becomes famous and meets his Mary Ann in the salons of the great. In the end he marries Mary Ann. This bold narrative may sound common- place, but as told by Mr. Zangwill it is full of humour and pathos. "Mary Ann, Merely" is a winsome little person despite externals, with a freshness and individuality which appeal to the onlooker of the comedy as they did to Lancelot, the v composer. She is artistically pourtrayed in Mr. ) Frohmann's Company this week by Miss Jessica / Solomon, an actress with a knowledge of character which finds a suitable outlet in the part. She makes Mary Ann all that she should be. Mr. Paul Lovett gives a clever study of Lancelot, the genius who wears long locks, strides the floor like a caged lion, has queer struggles between conscience and love and captivates the heart of Rosie, the landlady's dowdy daughter, as well as that of Mary Ann. He was just a little too frenzied at times, we thought, but there is comedy in frenzy, which we are, perhaps intended to laugh at. He was, at least, a typical genius. Peter, his friend, is a pleasing personality who could hardly have been better represented than he is by Mr. Stanley Logan • Miss Florence Radcliffe is excellent as the gushing Rosie, while Miss Violet Greville submits a perfect pmrtrayal of the voluble Cockney landlady. She was Robey-like in her make-up and gesture, and certainly provides half the fun. There is a large cast in which every part is well played, and we found "Merely Mary Ann" a very enjoyable comedy. The three acts which are performed in the lodging house are really excellent, but there was a hackneyed flavour about the last act, only relieved by the motor - mannerisms of Lord Tottenham (Mr. Herbeit Greville). NEXT, WEEK'S PROGRAMME. "MRS. PONDERBURY'S PAST."

Miss Emma Hutchison and Mr. Percy Hutchison whose management has been established for over 21 years, will on Monday next produce one of the funniest farces of the day, "Mrs. Ponderbury's Past," which ran for eight months to crowded houses at the Vaudeville Theatre, London. This makes the 33th London success produced by Ur. Percy Hutchinson, who has personally selected and rehearsed a powerful company including the clever comedian, Mr. George Mudie, in the leading part Mr. Ponderbury ; Mies Annie Stolwart, as Mrs. Ponderbury and Miss Florence Wix as Madame Modjiski. One has only to remember that the play is written by Sir Francis Burnand, the late Editor of Punch, to know that the dialogue i 9 sparkling with wit and that the play abounds with original farcical situations which cause roars of. laughter.

The annual general meeting of the Warrington CorpS of the St. John Ambulance Brigade was held at the Police Station last night week. There was a very large' attendance of members and amongst those present were Chief Surgeon J. Adams, Hon. Surgeon W. J. Collins, First Officer Williams, Sergeants Fell and Eanney and the Hon. Secretary, Mr. A. E. Spann. Chief Superin- tendent Captain Waring presided.—The Hon. Secretary submitted the annual report, which stated that the work of the past year had been of et successful character and progress had been made, go' All employers were asked to encourage their workpeople to gain a knowledge of first-aid work by joining the "*9 class which Dr. Adams had kindly promised to address, During the year first-aid had been rendered in upwards of 500 cases and, while thanking the business firms and it people of Warrington for the practical assistance hitherto given. It was suggested that a bigger sub- scription list would help the corps to carry forward a worthy cause.—The report and balance sheet were adopted, on the motion of Chief Surgeon Adams. Private W. H. James and Private T. Davies were re-elected auditors. Private T. Weston and Private A- Hankinson were elected as representatives upon the committee.—lt was decided that in future the weekly meetings of the members (for drill and practice) he' held on Wednesday night instead of on Friday night ae heretofore.—The Hon Secretary introduced the ques- tion of having a cycle squad in connection with the corps and the matter will be considered by the com- mittee.—A vote of thanks was passed to Chief Superintendent Waring for presiding, and also to the Hon. Surgeons for their presence.—lt was mentioned at the close of the meeting that the first aid class which is proposed to be held will commence on Wednesday next at elght o'clock at headquarters (the Police Station).
